# Changelog

## [Unreleased]

## 0.1.0-beta.32 (2026-11-10)

- Everything the agents make is now on the disk, apart from the data directory's own files. Each agent has one folder, `<data>/disk/agents/<agent>/` (its id in lower case, a dash, and 16 hex digits of its SHA-256). That folder holds the agent's learning folder (`learning/`) and its runs (`runs/<run>/`, each with `store/run.sqlite` and `work/`). store.sqlite and the server's files stay at the top of the data directory, as the control plane, and only the server opens them. A run's row in store.sqlite now holds its claim: `claim_epoch` and `claim_holder`. Each launch of the run's process bumps the epoch, with that process as the holder, and hands the process its epoch. Once the process is gone, the server takes the run back: it bumps the epoch, names itself the holder, and checkpoints the run's database. A run's database (pi-durable-disk's exclusive profile) is WAL, under an exclusive lock, with `wal_autocheckpoint = 0` and synchronous FULL. The run's process ends with its WAL in place: its store's close keeps the connection open (`keepWal`), and the process ends by process.exit, which never checkpoints. A test pins this node:sqlite behaviour, and its inverse: a close does checkpoint. The server reads a run's database with a fresh read-only, lock-free open (`vfs=unix-none`, EXCLUSIVE) per read. It opens the WAL only once the WAL holds a commit that SQLite's recovery would take: a frame with the header's salts, a valid chained checksum and a nonzero commit size. Until then it reads the database file alone, as an immutable file. A lock-free reader of a WAL whose first transaction is still being written recovers no frame, and its close removes that WAL, which loses the writer's rows. agentrun-pi exports that check as `walHasCommit`, and both readers use it. It never shows a read whose last entry is behind one it read before. It never opens the database for writing while a process holds the run. A data directory an earlier version wrote (runs under `<data>/runs/`, or a store of store version 1) is refused at start, in words, and nothing is migrated. pi-durable-agent's store is now store version 2. It names a run's folder by its row's agent (`runDir`, `runStorePath`), and adds `claimRun` and `runClaim`. A store of version 1 is refused. serve gains the local disk adapter: `mount`, `sync`, `unmount` and `revoke` for one machine, where a runner works in the folder itself and a revoke is the server's take-back of the claim. agentrun-pi's durable readers take `unlocked` (a fresh `vfs=unix-none` read, as the server's), which serve's reads of a run's decisions use. A run's folder is never made through a symbolic link: a link on the way to it refuses the run's start, and refuses an import that finishes a run its last import left without a transcript (pi-durable-agent `runFolders`).
- A workflow run's graph, once the run has ended, and the server's reads at the run's end (`workflowCarry` and `readWorkflowRun` of its outcome) now read the run's database lock-free, as every other server read does. They make no -wal or -shm file beside it, never wait on a lock, and never remove a WAL that holds commits. `GET /runs/{run_id}/graph` drops its `stale` field. The view it marked (the last whole view, or the live view, shown while the run's store was busy) can no longer happen, since a lock-free read never meets a busy store. The console no longer reads the field. pi-durable-agent's `readWorkflowRun(run, { unlocked })` and `workflowCarry(run, outcome, { unlocked })` take the unlocked read. agentrun-pi exports `withDurableFile`.
- The code taken from vercel-labs/durable-harness is now AgentRun's own: the port froze, and the pristine upstream copies, the named patches, the import tables, `scripts/port-durable-harness.mjs` and `npm run verify:port` are gone. `vendor/durable-harness/UPSTREAM.json` still records where each copy came from, and each copy keeps its header and NOTICE its attribution. With it go the kernel's paths that never run:
  - `AgentAdapter.version` and `AdapterCapabilities.fork` (no adapter forks; a node that asks for `context: "fork"` is refused as before).
  - `DriveContext.config`, and `requires`/`needs` on a host tool and an effect's options; `kernel/types.ts` is no longer pinned to upstream's types.
  - The Codex harness's `codex.spend` ledger and its dollar caps (`sandbox({ budget })`, `GatewayLedger`, the gateway handler's `budget`, `ledger` and `onCap`), which never fired, and its table of removed options.
  - `Box`, `BoxSpec`, `BoxedChild`, `Isolation` and `boxSlot` (`@parcha/agentrun-kernel/host`), which nothing implemented or used.
  - The model proxy's parts only tests used (`inputTokensMaxOf`, `matchRoute`, `parseStrict`, `reportedCostOf`) move to `@parcha/agentrun-kernel/testing`; `providerIdsOf` and `MCP_ROUTE_VERSIONS` leave the host API.
  - The box egress proxy (`startEgressProxy`, `parseAllow`, the address table) moves into agentrun-serve, its one user.

  Fixed: a run on Claude Code or Codex now closes its harness session when its process lets the run go, as the adapter's `close()` contract asks; before, serve never called it. On today's adapters an idle session's `close()` stops no process of its own (each drive already ends its CLI or app-server when its turn ends), so this keeps the contract for an adapter whose session holds one. For it, pi-durable-disk's `DoorHook` has a required `close()`: the door's stop calls it once, busy or idle (on a drain after the abort, on a fence alongside the kill, within the abort's bound). A hook with nothing of its own to stop resolves at once. `DoorHook` also has a required `harness` id: a `close()` that throws, rejects or overruns its bound is logged with it ("door close failed" / "door close timed out") through `serveRun`'s new optional `log`, which the run CLI passes its log line to. A harness session that lands after the door's close (a stop while it was still opening) is closed too, once.
- The agent's learning folder is written by one checked-in POSIX sh script (`learn/learning.sh`), run through the folder's Workspace as one exec per write. The script commits by compare and swap on main (a lost race exits 3 and writes nothing), brings the working tree in line, writes a merge's `.agentrun/diffs/<run>.json`, and writes `.agentrun/snapshot.json` last (snapshot v1). That covers a run's merge, a person's edit, create, retire, approve and restore, and the folder's first commit. The learning routes read only the snapshot, the bodies it names (each checked against its blob id, one retry, then 503) and the diff files, through the Workspace file API. They run no git and exec nothing, and every response keeps its shape. A later page's cursor reads at its main while the server still holds that snapshot, and otherwise answers as the first page of main now. A top-level `.md` that is no note (no front matter) is no longer served: it reads 404, and a retire by its blob gets 412.

  Upgrading: a learning folder made before this has no `.agentrun/snapshot.json`, so it reads as empty until its agent's next run starts. That start rebuilds the snapshot from main.
- An older server's learning records are no longer read: the reasons `no_write_tool`, `tool_denied`, `screen` and `screen_down` are gone from a run's learning reasons and the contract, and a ledger line with any of them is not counted in /v2/stats; a run's pin with only the index-only `learned` field gives the run nothing learned. Upgrading: runs recorded by a server before these were dropped show their old reason as stored and are not counted in the learning stats; nothing else to do.
- A run's seed is read with no git in the server process. The server runs the learning script's init, then its read at main (`learn/seed-check.ts` readSeed through `seedFrom`). When a body in the folder is not main's (an uncommitted edit), the seed reads git's objects at that commit, so a run still starts from main's commit and never from the working tree. Workflow drafts come from the folder's snapshot. A skill that holds a tracked link is left out with a one-line warning, and the other skills are laid. The learning script checks its own git floor (2.32): below it, a run starts with nothing learned and the script names the git it found. A link where the script would write is refused in the same words every learning read uses. Deleted from `learning.ts`: the host-git seed reads (`readSkills`, `readWorkflows`, `changedOrder`) and `learningBlocker`. Deleted from `learning-words.ts`: the stop words nothing says any more (`noGit`, `screenDown`, `notReviewed`). The end-of-run turn and propose still run on the server until the runner switch.

  Upgrading: nothing to do; the learning folder's layout and snapshot are unchanged.
- The store's schema version moves with the MCP access token's end, now kept by the store's own clock (`mcp_servers.token_until`): a data directory an earlier build wrote is refused at start, naming both versions, and never migrated. Start the server on a fresh data directory.
- A revision that names no place runs where `capabilities.places.default_place` says, worked out at each run the same way as at create. An agentic revision runs in the Docker box wherever this server has docker now, and on the runner where it does not. A workflow, and a revision whose tools name one the box refuses (`browser`), run on the runner. A save checks a revision with no place against where it would run, so `bash` with no place saves where docker is there. `capabilities.places.default` is gone: `default_place` is the one field for where a new agent goes and where a revision with no place runs (`x-agentrun-serve: "place/4"`). The console reads `default_place` for both, and an agent page with no place says it runs where the server's default place is.
  Upgrading: an agent saved with no place on a server that has docker now runs its next run in the Docker box, not on the runner. To keep it on the runner, save its definition with `"place": {"mode": "runner"}`. A client that read `places.default` reads `places.default_place.mode`.
- The pi family is 1.5.0 exactly (pi-durable, pi-ai, pi-coding-agent, chord), with one typebox (1.3.27); the older-pi CI legs are gone. A pi 1.5 run store is SQLite schema 2, which pi 1.3.0 or earlier cannot open again, so there is no rollback past this release. Tool results are under `output` only. A browser charge serve fails to count makes the run's spend unknown, and a capped run then refuses its next paid call until the spend is known again. The door's `GET /events` and a recording's live lines read through an adapter on pi 1.5's `observeCommits` (pi-durable-disk `./internals` `watchEvents`, with no compatibility promise) until they move to `conversation.watch`. A failed run store's close releases this process's live browser sessions again; a normal close keeps them for the next run. A claude-agent, codex or opencode run opens on pi 1.5, and an image it reads through pi's read tool is read. particle-1's cap hold keeps Particle's list price over pi-ai's promotional catalog price; a request is still booked at OpenRouter's billed total. The agent store reader reads pi-durable schema 2.
- A run's row in store.sqlite is now the whole lock on the run. Its `claim_epoch` changes only by compare-and-set. pi-durable-agent's `claimRun(runId, holder, from)` bumps the epoch only while the row still holds `from`; otherwise it is refused as `stale` and writes nothing. The new `setRunHolder(runId, epoch, holder)` names the holder under that epoch only. A holder is a name of at most 1,024 characters. serve's run process no longer writes `run.json` or `owner.lock`. It has no heartbeat and no lease watchdog. Its row names it instead: its pid, that process's start time, this boot and, once it serves, its door. The process prints the door on stdout, and the server writes it into the row. The process never opens store.sqlite. Its status (`sleeping`, `paused`, `done`, `failed`, with its detail) goes into its own run.sqlite, in pi-durable-disk's `RUN_STATUS_TABLE`. A take-back or a launch reads the row and kills any holder that still lives before it bumps the epoch. So no process of a lower epoch runs, or commits to the run's store, after the bump. A run whose holder is gone is taken over by its row. A stale run.json beside it, naming a live process, changes nothing. Before, that made the server adopt a process that was not the run's. pi-durable-disk's `openDurableRun` takes `epoch`, and `pi-durable-disk run` takes `--epoch N`. Both are for a run whose host holds its claim: no owner lock, no run.json, no heartbeat, no seal check. The run's record lives in its store, and the previous incarnation's record there is `previous`. The internals entry adds `RUN_STATUS_TABLE` and `processTable`.

  Upgrading: stop the server and let its runs' processes exit before you start this version. A run process an earlier version started is not adopted, because the run's row names no holder for it. Any run.json and owner.lock files left in run folders are ignored.
- Every run's box now has the run's `work/` at `/workspace`, where it had it at `/run/agentrun/run/work`; a tool or a prompt that named the old path names `/workspace`.
  A Docker box binds that one folder of the agent's disk (`<data>/disk/agents/<key>/runs/<run>/work`) and nothing else of the data directory.
  Under compose it gets the same folder as a subpath of the data volume.
  A Docker box is labelled with the epoch of its run's claim (`agentrun.epoch`).
  A run's process destroys every box of its run of an earlier epoch, or with none, before it makes its own or before a file tool works on the run's files; it never takes such a box over, and the model's next box tool answer starts, once, with "this run's box was replaced after a restart: /workspace is kept; packages installed outside it and /tmp are gone; reinstall what you need".
  A process that finds a box of a later epoch no longer holds the run, and refuses.
  A kept remote box (Daytona, the VM box) made with the old path is copied back from it, whole, then destroyed and made again at `/workspace`.
- Sign out signs out this browser only: `POST /v2/auth/sign-out` revokes its session on the server (each session now has an id, kept in the owner file as revoked until it would have ended, so a copy of that cookie stops working too) and clears its cookie, and leaves every other browser signed in. A session made before this change has no id: sign in once more after upgrading. The new `POST /v2/auth/sign-out-everywhere` ends every session on every browser and phone (what Sign out did before), and the console offers it as **Sign out everywhere**, below Sign out, after a confirm: "Signs out every browser and phone signed in to this server, this one too."
- **`fetch_page` is gone: grant `web_fetch` instead.** web_fetch's old name no longer saves, runs, maps to a permission or gets a Timeline label: a revision naming it is refused like any tool this server does not bind (`422 not_an_option` at its `tools[i]`), a workflow whose model or call node names it at `definition.workflow`, and the gate maps it to no settings key. Upgrading: an agent saved before this that still grants `fetch_page` is refused at its next run (`409 agent_not_runnable`, `details.unbound`) until it is saved again with `web_fetch`. `@parcha/pi-durable-agent` registers its fetch label and outcome for `web_fetch` alone.
- A trigger has no `overlap` field any more, in its view, `GET /v2/capabilities` and the OpenAPI document: a firing while its last run is active is always skipped, with why, which the docs state as its behaviour. `triggers.json` is read in this build's shape alone: a trigger missing `catchup_window_s`, `failures_in_a_row` or `disabled`, or firings kept under `run_now_kept`, are no longer read as defaults or copied over (shapes only unreleased builds wrote). Upgrading: a trigger's view and the capabilities no longer carry `overlap`; a firing while the last run is active is always skipped. Nothing to re-save.
- Each adapter states what its harness is, and serve reads that instead of the harness's name. `@parcha/agentrun-harness-claude/facts` and `@parcha/agentrun-harness-codex/facts` export `facts` (the kernel's `AdapterFacts`): the API its proxy speaks (`wire`), whether it takes a message mid-turn, the memory one run reserves, its thinking levels, its own web search (`native` or `live`), whether its model is its own, how it names a model, its install line and pins, the schemas its MCP client drops, whether it binds the session's MCP servers, an MCP tool alias, and its proxy policy. Serve keeps one table from a harness id to its entry and one `providerFor(wire, env, model)`: opencode and codex take one route because both speak the Responses API, and the gateway base rule is written once, so pi's runs take it too. Nothing else changes for a run, a capability or a refusal's words.
  Upgrading: an AGENT_MODEL_BASE_URL that is plain http to a non-loopback host, names a user, or has a query, fragment or dot segment is now refused on pi, as on Codex and OpenCode (a backslash or whitespace too); the refusal names the setting, and the gateway's key is never sent there. `startServe`'s `fauxHarness` option is gone; a test passes `harnessTable`, a module of harness table entries (`entries(builtins)`), in its place. `AGENTRUN_SERVE_FAUX_HARNESS` is no longer read; `AGENTRUN_HARNESS_TABLE` is set from `harnessTable` alone and dropped from the server's own environment.
- An agent can run unattended. Its definition takes `approvals`: `ask`, the default, is the tool gate as its settings say; `never` allows each tool that would ask instead, decided by the gate at each call, so a tool granted later and an MCP tool (`mcp:<server>/<tool>`, which asks by default) are covered on every harness. It never lifts a deny, whether set on the server or in the agent's own `tool_settings`, nor anything else the gate refuses before it decides between running and asking. A workflow's call node on a tool set to ask runs under `never`. The agent read serves `approvals`, its tools say an allow came from `unattended` (`permission_from`), and the run read serves the run's own `approvals`, as it started. A template apply's `overrides.approvals` sets it; an apply without it takes the package's own default (`approvals_default`: `never` for a template whose job runs on triggers, else `ask`). A value other than `ask` or `never` is a `422` naming `definition.approvals`. Upgrading: nothing to do; an agent that names none asks, as before.
- The owner watches a run's browser live from the console, read-only. `GET /v2/runs/{id}/browser` answers `{state: "live", viewer}` while the run's browser session is open (`viewer`, a link on this server with its token in the fragment), `{state: "none"}` when it has a browser with no session open, and `{state: "off", reason}` when it has none (no `browser` tool, or its browser resolves unavailable here); it takes the owner's password session in the console, never a token (`403`). Capabilities say so: `features.browser_live: {read_only: true}`.
  - The view is pi-browser's own viewer for every vendor, served by the run's process on a loopback port of its own: the vendors' own live-view links never leave that process. The process hands each session's viewer to the server in a file of that session's own in the run's directory, outside `work/` (`browser-live/<session>.json`, 0600, written whole), which goes when that session ends and never touches another session's; the token dies with the session, and the old link opens nothing.
  - The viewer's page and WebSocket are proxied under `/v2/runs/{id}/browser/live/` to that run's port alone: the page under `frame-ancestors 'self'` (the console keeps `'none'` everywhere else), the WebSocket from this server's own origin with the owner's session only. One viewer at a time (`viewer_in_use`).
  - The token is in no record, timeline row, run view, run file, home, log or the model's context.
  - `@parcha/pi-browser`'s live view takes `readOnly`: every input, navigation and dialog answer is dropped on the server (`held read_only`), `ready` says `readOnly: true`, and a watching viewer keeps no session open. A browser with no page yet (Kernel's headless one starts with none) tells the viewer `no_page`, and the page says "No page yet" until one opens.
- The owner takes over a run's browser from the live view and hands it back, and the agent can ask them to. Capabilities say so: `features.browser_live: {read_only: false, take_over: true, ask: true}`.
  - **Take over, hand back.** The viewer watches until the owner presses Take over in it; until then the run drops its input before the browser. The hold lasts until Hand back, the viewer is gone 2 minutes, or 15 minutes pass with no input. Meanwhile the agent's browser calls wait inside the tool, then go on with the page's old element ids dropped and the model told plainly that the person had the browser. Each change of hands moves the session's epoch, so an act a call took before it is refused. The run stays `working`; the timeline says "You took over the browser" and "You handed the browser back". A page's dialog that opens before the owner takes over never covers Take over; it says to take over to answer it, and its buttons stay off until they do.
  - **The agent asks.** `browser_request_handoff` is listed. The run reads `input_required`/`waiting` with `pending: {kind: "browser", instruction, completion_condition, asked_at, expires_at}`, a "Waiting for you: <instruction>" row, and `run.needs_input` with `pending.kind: "browser"`. The owner answers `POST /v2/runs/{id}/browser/hand-back` `{outcome: "done" | "cancelled"}` (their password session only): the first answer wins, a different later one is `409 handoff_answered`; unanswered for 15 minutes it expires. Handing the browser back in the viewer answers the agent's open ask on that session `done` too (first answer wins); a take-over that ends any other way (idle, away, the cap) leaves the ask open. An ask whose waiting row cannot be written fails, its tool's result saying so, with nothing held or left open, so asking again shows it. Each ask is its session's own: two conversations of a run that pick the same request id make two asks, each with its own instruction, answer and take-over.
  - **The owner's time counts.** A take-over and an ask hold 15 minutes of session time at the provider's price under the run's cap before they start (once per session: a take-over and an ask on it share one hold); with no room they are refused in the cap's words (in the viewer, or in the tool's result). A take-over that input keeps alive is held again every 15 minutes, and with no room the browser goes back to the agent at the cap. When the browser comes back, the time the person had stays held until the session's next charge books it. An ask a restarted run's process takes up holds only what is left of it, up to its expiry, and none once it is past.
  - A run's process that dies while the agent's ask waits is started again, and the new process takes the wait up: the owner's answer reaches the agent, or the ask expires. That holds when the owner answers after the process went and before the server found it gone: the hand-off is started again until its call's result is saved, the answer already on disk.
  - A browser session a restarted run's process keeps gets its viewer handed over again: the owner no longer reads `none` while it is live.
  - **The viewer's code is the server's own.** Serve now serves the viewer's page, script and style from the pi-browser it has installed, never from the run's port; only the viewer's WebSocket goes to the run.
  - `@parcha/pi-browser`'s live view takes `takeOver: { take(tag) }`, the driving gate gains `take`, `give`, `held`, `epoch`, `setPresent`, `untilFree`, `command` and `settling` (with `holdIdleMs`, `holdAwayMs` and `settleMs`: a hand-back wakes the agent only once the person's last command has returned, or `settleMs` has let it go, after which it no longer renews the person's turn), and `viewerAssets` is exported from `@parcha/pi-browser/live-view`.
  - The timeline's "Waiting for you" row for a browser hand-over (`agent.handover`) takes the host's redact, as a tool call's arguments do: a held value the model echoes in what it asks never shows; the run's `pending` for a browser ask is redacted the same way.
- A plain `npx @parcha/agentrun` runs a Claude Code agent with nothing else installed: Claude Code installs itself on first use. AgentRun still never ships the Claude Agent SDK. The first run on claude-agent, or the first agent saved on it, installs the version AgentRun pins (`@anthropic-ai/claude-agent-sdk@0.3.286`) from npm into `<data>/harnesses`, with the npm that runs the server, no key of the server's in npm's environment and no install script. The install is pinned by a lock the server ships (`harnesses/claude-agent/`, the package.json and npm's lock for it, every package of the tree by sha512, which `npm ci` holds each tarball to from whichever registry npm is configured for), and it lands whole or not at all: npm installs into a fresh directory, the result is checked there, then renamed into place and made read-only. Every launch of a claude-agent run waits for that install first (its start, a recovery after a restart, a relaunch, a wake), so no run starts on a missing SDK, and a run a crash left waiting is installed for and resumed by the next server. A run waiting for the install keeps its place under `max_concurrent_runs` and the memory limit until it launches, and gives it back at once if it is cancelled or fails. It is one install at a time: every run that needs it waits for that one, its ready time counted from the install's end, and shows a `phase` row, `Installing Claude Code (first use)…`, while it does. Each claude-agent run then loads the SDK, its CLI and the MCP library from `<data>/harnesses` and nowhere else, never a copy beside the server or a global one. Until it is installed, `GET /v2/capabilities` lists claude-agent with `installs: "on_first_use"`. Each piece loads only from its own package inside `<data>/harnesses`, never from a `node_modules` above it, and a tree that is not whole is installed again; a server that stops mid-install stops its npm. When npm fails, the run fails and its error names the command that installs it by hand, the same locked install: the shipped package.json and lock copied into a fresh `<data>/harnesses.new`, `npm ci --ignore-scripts --include=optional` there, then that directory put in place of `<data>/harnesses`. claude-agent's npx hint (`npx -p … -p @anthropic-ai/claude-agent-sdk@0.3.286 …`) is gone, and codex's and OpenCode's npx lines no longer name the SDK.
  Upgrading: Claude Code installs itself on first use.
- A call that never started now runs on resume instead of reading "interrupted". When a claude-agent run resumes a transcript that ends in a host-tool call with no result, and the run's journal holds no intent for that call (the host's gate parked it for a person's approval, or the process died before the call was written), the call now goes through the host's gate again under the id the model gave it, runs once, and its result goes into the transcript before the CLI starts again. Before, the CLI answered such a call "[Tool call interrupted]". A call whose intent is in the journal is never run again: the CLI answers it as before, and the recovery note says what the log knows of it. A gate that parks again writes nothing.
  - `@parcha/agentrun-kernel`: `SessionIO.gate` (the host's gate before a host-tool call's intent: allow, deny in the host's words, or park), the tool bridge's `gate` option, and `resumeUnstartedCalls` / `unstartedCalls` in `./process`, the decision every CLI harness shares on a resume. `AdapterFacts.rerunsStarted` (false for a harness that never runs a started call again).
  - `@parcha/agentrun-harness-claude`: the gate is asked only once the assistant entry that made the call is in the log, so a parked call is found dangling on the resume under the same id.
  - `@parcha/agentrun-serve`: the harness conformance suite skips the cases an adapter's facts rule out (`factsRuleOut`), saying they are by design. The claude-agent subject runs through the runner and passes the park-and-resume cases.
- `@parcha/agentrun` ships an Agent Skills folder, `skills/agentrun-cli`, so a coding agent can drive the server through the CLI. Its `SKILL.md` covers how the CLI finds the server and the token, five recipes as shell blocks (make an agent from a definition file, start a capped run and wait, answer a run that asks, read what it wrote and what it cost, raise a cap or approve a call), the exit codes and one line per command group. `references/commands.md` has one entry per command with its usage, summary, flags and route. Both are generated with the command table by `node scripts/generate-clients.mjs`, and their header holds the table's hash. `--check` fails when either differs from the generated files, or when the skill names a command or flag the CLI does not have. Recipe 5 stops a run at a cap too small for it (exit 8), raises the cap with `agentrun runs raise-cap RUN_ID --max-cost-usd N` and waits for the run to complete. The generator picks that command from the table by its operation. A tool call's approval is answered by the person in the console, since the server takes that answer from no token, and the recipe says so.
  `agentrun skill install [--claude] [--codex] [--dir DIR]` copies the skill to `~/.claude/skills/agentrun-cli` and `~/.agents/skills/agentrun-cli` (the shared folder Codex reads), by default to each of the two whose folder exists, or to `DIR/agentrun-cli`. It prints the paths it wrote. It refuses (exit 2, nothing touched) a target that is the package's own skill folder, through a link too, lies inside it or holds it, and it replaces an older copy of the skill by copying beside it and renaming, so a failed copy leaves the old one in place.
  `agentrun tool-keys set KEY_ID FIELD [--from-env VAR]` saves one field of a tool key. The value is read from stdin or from the named environment variable, never from the command line, and appears in no output or error. A `KEY_ID` that is empty, `.` or `..` is refused, and the request is sent only when its URL, the base URL's path included, is the tool key route.
  `agentrun login [--base-url URL]` reads a token from stdin and refuses one typed on a terminal. It checks the token against the server (a refused token is exit 4 and nothing is written), then writes `base_url` and `api_key` to `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/agentrun/config.json` (else `~/.config/agentrun/config.json`) with mode 0600 in a 0700 directory. `agentrun logout` removes the file. A token that is not a valid header value is refused before anything is sent, exit 4.
  Every command refuses a base URL that is not http or https or has a query, a fragment, a user or a password (exit 2), from `--base-url`, `AGENTRUN_BASE_URL` or the config file: such a URL could swallow the path appended to it, so another route answered. `login` saves the token only when the check's answer is the agents list.
  A command that sends a secret (`login`, `tool-keys set`, `tool-keys save`, `mcp-servers save|preview`, and `api` to their routes) never prints the server's error body: a refusal prints its status, its `error.code` and a message of the CLI's own, with the same exit code as before. As a second line against a server that echoes what it was sent, a secret's copies in what it does print, as it is, JSON-escaped or URL-encoded, are cut out: from a JSON answer's string values and keys, so the answer printed is still JSON with its structure intact, and from the text of any other answer. Every string value of such a body is a secret except the public fields `scripts/clients/resources.json` names by full path (an MCP server's `url` and `auth.type`, a preview's `name`), which `generate-clients --check` holds to the body's schema. A header's value is always a secret, whatever the header is called.
  An MCP server's OAuth sign-in has `agentrun mcp-servers oauth-start NAME`, its body (with any client secret) from stdin or a file, and printing the page to open with its URL, scopes and client id intact, and `mcp-servers oauth-client` reads this server's client metadata document. The callback, which only the owner's browser reaches, has no command.
  The `agentrun-serve` skill and the server's `llms.txt` point to the CLI skill for everything the MCP tools do not cover.
- Agents on codex call a saved MCP server's tools: grant `mcp:<server>/<tool>` in `definition.tools`, as on pi and claude-agent, and `features.mcp_client.harnesses` now lists codex. The harness writes each granted server in Codex's `mcp_servers` itself, at the session's model proxy (`/mcp/<server>`, behind the session key), with only the granted tools enabled and approve mode, so Codex never holds the credential. The proxy records each call in the session before it sends it, by Codex's own call id, so a call a crash cut off is answered interrupted and never sent again. A server that does not start fails the turn (`required`). The Codex proxy policy admits the granted tools in the `mcp__<server>` namespace Codex 0.164 sends them in, and the timeline reads `<server> · <tool>`. The kernel's model proxy mounts `/mcp/<server>` itself, once for every harness with an MCP client of its own, from the session's MCP and the client fact its adapter declares (`capabilities().mcpClient`, the `_meta` key of a call's id: `claudecode/toolUseId` on Claude Code, `callId` on Codex); `ModelProxyOptions.mcp` takes `{ session, client }` in place of a handler.
- A run's live view is also served as the computer family, one for every computer kind, beside the browser's own routes. `GET /v2/runs/{id}/computer` (getRunComputer) answers `{state: "live", kind, viewer, hold}`, `{state: "none", kind, hold}` or `{state: "off", hold, why}`; `kind` is `browser`, `desktop` or `phone` (an open viewer's own, as its row in the store says it), and `hold` says who drives (`agent`, `pending`, `person`). Its viewer is under `/v2/runs/{id}/computer/live/`, with the same page, CSP, origin and cookie checks as `/browser/live/`, served by the same code. `GET /runs/{id}/browser`, `/browser/hand-back` and `/browser/live/` answer as before.
- An agent's computer is one setting, `computer: { kind, provider? }`, with kind `none`, `sandbox`, `desktop`, `phone` or `browser`. The v2 `place` stays readable and writable as its view (`{mode: "runner"}` is `{kind: "none"}`, `{mode: "sandbox", box}` is `{kind: "sandbox", provider: box}`), and writing either sets both. With no setting, Mode 1 runs Docker's sandbox where Docker is ready and none otherwise; Mode 2 runs none.

  Settings → Computers lists what the server offers: each kind with its providers, their capabilities, and whether each declared key is saved, never its value. A provider's keys are saved by the names it declares, sealed in the store, and never echoed back. "Test connection" makes one account-level read that never creates a computer, and gives `ok` or the provider's words. At a run's claim the setting is checked against the server's mode and the provider's keys: Mode 1 runs a sandbox only on Docker, Mode 2 refuses Docker's sandbox, and a desktop, a phone or a browser is allowed in both. A provider with a required key missing is refused in words that name Settings → Computers. A run's read gains `computer: {kind, provider, state, why?}`.

  A run's tools follow its computer's kind. A desktop adds `desktop_screen` and `desktop_input`; a phone adds `phone_screen`, `phone_tree`, `phone_tap`, `phone_swipe`, `phone_type`, `phone_key`, `phone_apps` and `phone_open`; a browser adds pi-browser's browser tools; each comes after the file and shell tools. The run's folder is mounted in the computer only when the kind has files and exec and its provider mounts the folder: a sandbox always, and a desktop when its provider does. Otherwise bash runs in the run's folder on the runner, asking first. Screenshots come back as images. `phone_tree` lists what on the phone's screen can be tapped or typed into, from the screen's structure, each node with its center and bounds in screenshot pixels, at most 150 nodes and 12,000 characters (a last line counts any more); `phone_key` presses back, home or enter. Both are under the "phone" permission. A run whose computer is a desktop or a phone gets that kind's skill (`desktop-computer` or `phone-computer`) in its `.agents/skills/`, beside the agent's own; an agent skill of the same name is kept and the kind's is not laid. The background tools' refusal with no computer now names the setting.

  A run's desktop or phone can be watched and taken over by its owner. `GET /v2/runs/{id}/computer` gives its kind, its state (`live`, `none` or `off`, with `why` when there is nothing to watch), whether the owner holds it, and the provider's live stream of its screen (view-only while the agent works). `POST /v2/runs/{id}/computer/take-over` gives the owner a stream that drives it and parks the agent's screen, input and phone calls; `POST .../hand-back` gives it back. At the hand-back a parked look runs on the screen as the owner left it, and a parked act is not done: the agent is told the person had the computer and to look again. A take-over goes back to the agent on its own after 15 minutes, or at the run's end, and the run's read says `taken_over` while it lasts. These routes are owner-only, as a run's browser view is: they take the console's password session, never a token. The browser keeps its own routes.
- A coding agent gets `agents create` and `runs start` right on the first try, from the help and the 422s. The served OpenAPI document gives a working request body for `POST /agents`, `POST /agents/{agent_id}/runs` and `POST /triggers`, as the JSON media type's `examples` (examples/1), and `npx @parcha/agentrun agents create --help`, `runs start --help` and `triggers create --help` end with it as a `--body '…'` line to paste. A test sends every documented example to the server and requires a 2xx, so none can stop working.
  - Each field issue of a `422 input_invalid` (`error.fields[]`) can gain `example` (explain/1), a value the field accepts: the operation's documented body's value at that field, else the schema's example, const or option, else one made from its type (`true` for a switch, the least number it takes). A value is suggested only once the route's own check of the body takes it in place, so a 422 never suggests a value the server refuses: `definition.id: "bad id"` is told `e.g. "due-diligence"`, and an invalid `definition.model` gets no example. The message says it too: `definition.learning is true or false, e.g. true`, `input is an object, e.g. {"content":"Research https://stripe.com and write the brief."}`, `definition.name is required, e.g. "Due diligence"`. A run's `input` is told from the input this server checks: an agentic run's `{content}`, a workflow run's its workflow's input schema (`{"question":"…"}` for a workflow that requires `question`), never the other's; a run pinned to a `revision` is told from that revision's, and a pin that names no revision gets no hint.
  - A field sent at the wrong level, which an object inside the body keeps, is told where it belongs: `{"name": …}` to `POST /agents` answers `The body keeps no field name: name belongs under definition.`, with `fix` `name belongs under definition: {"definition":{"name":"Due diligence"}}` and that body as its `example`.
  - Upgrading: a field issue's `message` may now end with `, e.g. <value>` or name where the field belongs, and the issue may carry `example`; a client that compares a field issue's message whole compares its `field` and `reason` instead.
- A new agent in the default Docker box that names no tools gets the background tools with its shell: `bash`, `bash_background`, `bash_output`, `bash_input`, `bash_signal` and `bash_list`. It can start a command that outlives one call (a dev server, a watcher, a long build) from its first run. A definition that names its tools, an empty list included, keeps exactly those.
  - The README says what the background tools do not track: a child that leaves the command's process group (its own `setsid`, a daemon) is not counted, signalled or ended at the run's end. Only the box's end or its 24-hour lifetime ends it; the box's process limit only bounds how many processes there can be.
  - The tool gate holds each background tool to `bash`'s setting, and the README and the source say so.
  - A process whose runner ended before it did reads as gone, whether its box was replaced or its runner was killed.
- AgentRun's container image runs the server and its runners: the default command is the server with one runner beside it; with runners of their own, the server takes `--no-runner` and each runner is the same image with `runner --data /data --name <name> [--loops N]`, against the same `/data` volume, so a runner always knows the schema of the store its server set up. `deploy/compose.yaml` runs a server and two runners on one store, and `deploy/README.md` says how to run more.
  - The image is on Node 24 (`node:24.21.0-trixie-slim`, pinned by digest), the Node the workspace needs, and carries the computer daemon's Linux binaries (`computerd-linux-amd64` and `-arm64` in `@parcha/agentrun-computer`'s dist), built with Go 1.24.13 in the build stage only.
  - The seal key comes from the operator: the server and every runner must be given the same `AGENTRUN_SEAL_KEY` (`openssl rand -base64 32`). A container given none makes its own, which no other container shares.
  - A runner starts after the server is healthy (only the server sets the store up; a runner on a store it does not know exits 3), and its health is `runner --check --data /data --name <name>`. On SIGTERM it hands its runs back at once and gives up a stuck loop at 25 s: give it 35 s to stop.
  - `deploy/walk/smoke.sh` (CI's `deploy-compose` job) and `deploy/walk/reclaim.sh` walk the stack on the faux model: a run completes on a runner, and a run whose runner gets SIGTERM or SIGKILL mid-run is taken over by the other runner and completes with each step done once.
  - No upgrade in place: a `/data` volume from an earlier server holds its old store in `store.sqlite`, which the new server refuses at start. Keep the earlier version on that volume, or start on a new data directory and re-create the agents.
- Connect GitHub end to end: a server makes its one GitHub App with GitHub's app-manifest flow, a `github` trigger source runs on it, and its runs get GitHub tokens from it. `POST /v2/integrations/github/connect` answers the manifest form the console posts to GitHub (`action` with the state in its URL, the public hook `/v2/hooks/github`, its callback and setup URLs, pull requests write and contents, metadata and issues read, the `pull_request` and `issue_comment` events); the callback converts GitHub's code and keeps the App's private key and webhook secret sealed (never its client secret), and the installed redirect records an installation GitHub confirms to the App's JWT. Both browser legs send the browser to the console at the public URL, `<public>/#/settings/github`, `github=connected` or `github=error&reason=state_invalid|conversion_failed|installation_unverified`. `GET /v2/integrations/github` shows `{app_id, slug, installations}`; `DELETE` disconnects it, turning every github trigger off and keeping them, and names the App's settings page on GitHub to delete it there. Refusals are `409 github_no_public_url` (it needs `AGENTRUN_PUBLIC_URL`), `github_no_seal_key`, `github_already_connected` and, for a github trigger with no App, `github_not_connected`. A trigger `{kind: "github", accept: [...]}` keeps no secret: one signed event fires every github trigger whose accept takes it, each its own firing (keyed by the body's SHA-256) and run, with the installation and repository on the payload's tag; an ignored body keeps its key for the day, since GitHub does not sign `X-GitHub-Event`. An installation deleted or suspended leaves the list, and the github triggers turn off (`disabled.reason: "connection_lost"`) only when none is left. An MCP server saved with `auth: {type: "github_app"}` gets, at each launch of a run (start and every resume), an installation token narrowed to the run's one repository (its event's, or the one its input names), beside the server's static headers, in memory only and cut from whatever a run shows; it is not renewed inside a live run (a call past its hour is answered that it expired, and the next resume mints a fresh one). A server-wide pushed source may now fan one event out to every trigger that takes it, a trigger's own hook is checked by its own source, and two sources on one hook route refuse to start. Upgrading: nothing to do.
- Codex installs itself on first use, as Claude Code does, through one table of the harnesses that install on first use (`src/harness-install.ts`). The first run on codex, or the first agent saved on it, installs the version AgentRun pins (`@openai/codex@0.164.0`, with this platform's binary package) from npm into `<data>/harnesses/codex`; Claude Code's install moves to `<data>/harnesses/claude-agent`: an install an earlier build left at the top of `<data>/harnesses` (its package.json, lock and node_modules, read-only) is removed before the first install, with one log line, and Claude Code installs again; a `harnesses` directory that holds anything else is left as it is. Each is pinned by a lock the server ships (`harnesses/codex/`, every package by sha512) and installs the same way: `npm ci --ignore-scripts --include=optional` into a fresh directory, checked there (for Codex, that its platform package and binary landed, whatever the person's npm config omits), renamed into place and made read-only; a server that stops mid-install stops its npm. A codex run waits for the install and shows `Installing Codex (first use)…` while it does; until it is installed, `GET /v2/capabilities` lists codex with `installs: "on_first_use"`. Codex runs only the binary in that tree, never a copy beside the server; when npm fails, the run's error names the command that installs it by hand. Codex's npx hint (`-p @openai/codex@0.164.0`) is gone. The server image no longer carries a Codex CLI: its build removes `@openai/codex`, its platform packages and its bin link, and `docker/image-check.sh` refuses an image that holds any of them. The hint that restarts AgentRun with OpenCode keeps how it was started: npx's `-y`, `--port` and `--no-open`. `node scripts/harness-locks.mjs` (formerly `claude-lock.mjs`) makes both locks again. `@parcha/agentrun-harness-codex` takes the binary a host installed (`binary: { path, packageVersion }`, checked against the pins) and exports `codexTarget()`.
  Upgrading: Codex installs itself on first use, and the server image no longer carries a Codex CLI. Claude Code installs again on its first run, under `<data>/harnesses/claude-agent`; an earlier install at the top of `<data>/harnesses` is removed.
- The last data serve kept by a harness's name moves to the adapters' facts: a CLI's own tools and the table tool each one is (`AdapterFacts.nativeTools`, stated by claude-agent and codex), the harnesses each model key runs (from their wires' vendors; OpenRouter's key runs every one) and OpenRouter's default model for each wire's vendor. Nothing changes for a run, a capability or a refusal's words.
- A run's store can now run at `synchronous = NORMAL` and still never repeat a side effect after a box is lost. `syncNow(store, BARRIER.<name>)` makes every commit the store has made durable with one small commit at FULL. A FULL commit syncs the write-ahead log, so it makes every earlier commit durable too, not only its own. The barriers and the stores they guard are listed in one table (`barriers.ts`). A test holds that table to the source: every barrier call is named and in its listed file, every store that is opened is listed, a store with no effects runs no tool or model, and no barrier may still be planned once the run's store runs at NORMAL.
  - The run's store still opens at FULL (`JOURNAL_SYNCHRONOUS`), so nothing is synced twice yet. `openArchilStore` and `openDurableRun` take `synchronous: "NORMAL"` for a store that should run at NORMAL.
  - A pi tool call that is not replay-safe (or a billed replay-safe one) goes out only after the server's tool gate synced the store, once pi-durable has committed its intent. A kernel effect (claude-agent, codex, opencode) that is not replay-safe runs only after its start record is synced: `SessionWriter` has an optional `sync()`, and `serialWriter` passes it on.
  - A non-pi harness's model request leaves only once the server's start mark for it is committed and synced. `ModelPortOptions.onEvent` may return a promise for `request_start`; the runner awaits it, and a rejection refuses the request, which is booked withdrawn at $0 and never taken for an open one.
  - A run syncs its store after each status it writes and when it is released. The server's checkpoint of a run it takes back stays at FULL, whatever level the run's store runs at.
- A run's learning says the notes it was given (`given`: each note its `# Learned` section names, whole or as an index line) and the notes it read (`read`: the note files it opened with the read tool), and each note on the agent's learning view counts its runs of each (`runs: {given, read}`). Whether a run applied a note is not measured. Upgrading: a run's learning field `used` is now `read`, and `given` is new.
- An owner can allow named private-network hosts for an MCP server's OAuth sign-in, in Settings → MCP servers → Private network hosts (`GET`/`PUT /v2/settings/mcp-servers/oauth/private-hosts`). The list is empty by default, so an authorization server on a private network is refused as before, now in words that name the host and where to allow it. A listed host (exact match, at most 32, no wildcards, ranges, ports or schemes) may resolve to 10/8, 172.16/12, 192.168/16, 100.64/10 or fc00::/7, still only over https; loopback, link-local (cloud metadata), unspecified, multicast and broadcast addresses, and this machine's and this server's own, stay refused whatever the list says. The server's sign-in and a runner's token refresh read the same setting from the store. Changing it takes the owner's password session in the console: a token's `PUT` is 403. A runner refresh the address check refuses is no longer reported as a sign-in server that didn't answer: its calls are refused unsent in the check's words.
  Settings → MCP servers also: a saved server's row, and each MCP tool in the agent editor's picker, says "not metered" where the server's read lists the tool in `not_metered`; the Change form no longer sends `prices` (the server keeps the saved ones); a failed sign-in reads in words, never its error code; and a server behind OAuth shows one Sign in button after a Preview. The keys page's headings read "Model keys" and "Tool keys", the names the console's and the server's words use. A saved MCP server's 422 reasons (`redirect`, `not_https`, `too_many_tools`, `tools_too_large`, `reserved`, `expired`) are listed in the document's InputIssue.
  A saved MCP server's read gains `secret: { name, set, source, changed_at }`, its credential as every secret is read: `source` `console` for a pasted token or headers, `sign-in` for an OAuth sign-in, null for none; `changed_at` moves only when a new credential is saved or signed in (never on a token refresh). The console's MCP page shows both.
- An MCP tool's setting may pin arguments: `pin: {<argument>: <JSON scalar>}` on `mcp:<server>/<tool>` (never the wildcard), in `PUT /v2/settings/tools` and in an agent's `definition.tool_settings`, 1 to 8 top-level arguments, each a JSON scalar (`null`: left out) or a list of 1 to 16 of them, the argument one of the list. The tool gate refuses a call whose arguments are not as pinned before it is sent or asked about, whatever the permission, and the model reads "mcp:slack/slack_send_message may only be called with channel_id = C0123: its destination is fixed by the agent's settings". A call approved under ask is checked again before it runs. A pin in `tool-settings.json` that does not read refuses every call of its tool, is served as `pin_broken: true`, and stays broken through any save that keeps its key without a valid pin. The agent's pin and the server's both hold (an argument both pin takes what holds both); where nothing holds both, every call is refused. A template's `mcp` entry takes `pin: {<tool>: {<argument>: "<value or ${parameters.x}>"}}`: apply writes it, filled, into the agent's tool settings, and a pin whose parameter was not given sets the tool to deny with a `pins.<tool>` warning. `GET /v2/templates` shows each tool's `pin`. `market-monitor` pins its Slack and Gmail sends to its `channel`, and `inbound-lead` its Slack send to `slack_channel`, so a granted send can only go to the destination the owner set at apply; `code-review` pins `pull_request_review_write` to `method` one of `create`, `submit_pending`, `delete_pending` and `event` absent or `COMMENT`, so it can never approve, request changes or resolve a thread.

  Until the agent store's cutover an agent's own pin is refused at `POST /v2/agents` (`422 not_set_up_here`, pointing at `/v2/settings/tools`), and a template apply writes no pin and warns `pins.<tool>` with the pin to set on the server; the server's pin holds every run today.
- A node naming `provider: "parallel"` runs with only an OpenRouter key. With `PARALLEL_API_KEY` it uses Parallel's own API, as before; without it, it runs on Parallel's model through OpenRouter (`parallel/decisions-fast`), on the OpenRouter key the server already reads. The same evidence on both routes: its web citations (OpenRouter's `url_citation` annotations), and its cost is what OpenRouter reports. `workflows.decisions.providers` gives parallel's `route` (`"parallel"` or `"openrouter"`); the mark is `decisions/3`. With neither key, the refusal names both. OpenRouter's Decisions provider now reads any model's `url_citation` annotations as evidence (its capability is `citations`), and books a reported cost only above 0: a $0 report, from a model OpenRouter's catalog prices at 0, is an unknown cost, never $0. Upgrading: a client that validated `workflows.decisions` against `decisions/2` refuses the new `route` field.
- In a sandbox, `read` opens an image (png, jpg, gif or webp) at any absolute path in the run's box, such as a frame rendered to `/tmp`, and returns it to the model as an image of at most 3.75 MiB, so that as base64 it fits the 5 MiB the strictest platform takes. The extension and the file's bytes must both say image. The file is read through the box, never from the server, and the box sends at most one byte past the bound, so a larger file is refused without the server ever holding it. On another harness (claude-agent, Codex, OpenCode) the image reaches the model as an image too. Any other file outside `work/` is still refused, as is any path on the server. `write` and `edit` stay in `work/`, and a runner-placed agent is unchanged.
- A run and an agent read over the API carry their page in the console, `console_url` (`consoleurl/1`): every run the API answers (`POST /v2/agents/{agent_id}/runs`, `GET /v2/runs/{run_id}`, the lists' items) has `<origin>/#/runs/<run_id>`, and every agent `<origin>/#/agents/<agent_id>` (an agent whose id is `new` gets its Runs tab, `<origin>/#/agents/new/runs`, as the console links it, since `#/agents/new` is the new-agent form). The origin is the address people open: the server's public URL (`AGENTRUN_PUBLIC_URL`, or Render's `RENDER_EXTERNAL_URL`) when it has one, else the address the request reached the server at (its `Host`, so a caller through a tunnel or a forwarded port gets a link on that port), else the server's own address. A retry with the same `Idempotency-Key` gets the page at its own request's address. A client gives a person that link instead of guessing the server's port. MCP's `runs_get` answers the route's `console_url` for the caller's own `Host`. `npx @parcha/agentrun runs start` and `runs get` print it with the run.
  Upgrading: nothing to do; the field is new. MCP's `runs_get` link is the same as before, except that with a public URL set it is now at the public URL rather than at the address the caller used.
- A runner's close drains: `startRunner`'s `drainMs` lets each run it holds go on to its own end (an answer, a park, a person's stop) for up to that long before it stops the rest where they resume, so a turn in flight finishes and is kept instead of being cut and sent again, paid twice, on the next start. Nothing is claimed once the close begins; claims are renewed and a run's commands (a person's stop) still reach it meanwhile. The default, 0, stops every run at once as before.
- A secret is saved only from the owner's session, and the server never starts without a seal key. Saving, replacing or removing a tool key or a model key (`PUT` and `DELETE /v2/settings/tool-keys/{key_id}`) takes the owner's password session (accounts on), or, on a server bound to loopback alone with accounts off, the console the launch link opened; the API token is refused (403) in one sentence on every server, loopback included, and a server bound off loopback with accounts off takes no secret at all. One check in the dispatch decides it, from a registry each kind of secret adds its write routes to; the API document marks every secret field `x-agentrun-secret: true`, and a test fails for a marked route that skips the check. `GET /v2/auth/session` now answers with accounts off too: `accounts`, `secrets_writable`, and `secrets_reason` (`sign_in`, `no_accounts` or `token`) when it is false. Every secret's view answers one shape, `SecretMeta` (name, set, source: env, console or sign-in, changed_at), never a value: an object holding one secret carries it as `secret`, one holding several as `secrets`, each named `<plane>:<id>`. Each row of `GET /v2/settings/tool-keys` now has `secrets`, one per field, named `tool:<FIELD>` or `model:<FIELD>`, with the time that field's own value was saved. With no `AGENTRUN_SEAL_KEY`, the server makes one at its first start in `agentrun/seal.key` under `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME` (`~/.config`), 0600 in a 0700 directory, printing only its path, by the launcher's rules; a key or directory another user can read, a linked directory, or nowhere to keep one stops the start, in words. The runner reads the same key: it no longer falls back to an all-zero key. The CLI has no command for an operation that writes a secret: `agentrun tool-keys set`, `tool-keys save` and `tool-keys delete` are gone, and the generated CLI leaves out every operation the API document marks `x-agentrun-secret` (a body field, or the operation whole, as a removal is); its help says keys and other secrets are set in the console, signed in. The API keeps the routes, and the TypeScript client keeps `toolKeys.save` and `toolKeys.delete`. Upgrading: a script that saved keys with the token now gets a 403; save them in the console, or set them where the server starts (the environment always wins).
- A run's spend cap prices each model request's input from the previous request's cache (`cap_mode` `expected`, the default): the input the previous request of its conversation read from or wrote to the cache counts at the cache-read price when it ended less than 4 minutes before, so a cap pays for about what the run really spends instead of pausing near half of it. Output never passes the cap; a cache miss can, by at most the request's bound, served as `cost.overshoot_bound_usd` while it is in flight (and as `pending.overshoot_bound_usd` on a run paused at its cap). The real cost is booked, one timeline row says `Went $X over its cap on a cache miss (at most $Y).`, and the run pauses at its cap instead of failing. `cap_mode: "strict"`, on a run's body or an agent's definition, keeps the worst case: every input token uncached.
- A run near its spend cap is told once to finish: when what is left is under three times its typical request (the median of the run's own model's priced requests after the first), the model's next request carries "About $X left of this run's budget: finish now with your answer.", and the Timeline shows it as the server's row. On pi it goes into that request's context; on claude-agent, codex and opencode the model proxy adds it to the request's body after everything the CLI sent, so the cached prefix is unchanged. No message is queued, so nothing runs after the model's answer. Once per cap, across restarts; a raise re-arms it.
- The published image runs Docker boxes with a network, and `compose.yaml` at the repository's root is the one way to run it. Use one command on a Linux host with Docker Engine 26 or later: `DOCKER_GID=$(stat -c %g /var/run/docker.sock) docker compose up -d`. It starts the server with its data on a named volume and the host's Docker socket. Each run's `bash` then runs in a Docker box beside the server, which reaches the hosts on its list through the run's egress proxy and nothing else. Before this, every box create in a container failed, because the box was bound to a path that only the server's container had. At start, a server in a container now reads its own container's id from `/proc/self/mountinfo` and asks Docker for its mounts. It then mounts each box's run `work/` and proxy directory (`<data>/box-egress/`) as subpaths of the named volume that holds the data directory. Any other layout makes docker unavailable with one reason: `run AgentRun in a container with docker compose up from compose.yaml (its named data volume is how boxes reach a run's files)`. An older engine gets `Docker Engine <26 can't mount a named volume's subpath: upgrade to Docker 26+`. The image now has the Docker client (Debian's `docker-cli`) and no daemon. `packages/serve/docker/compose.yaml` is gone, replaced by the root `compose.yaml`, and the README's `docker run` is replaced by compose. pi-transport's Docker provider takes `paths`, which turn a mount below `at` into that named volume's subpath. A server on a host makes its boxes exactly as before. The socket is root on the host, and the README says so.
- A run paused at its spend cap serves `pending.least_cap_usd`: the least cap that sends the refused request, the very figure
  its words name ("Raise the cap to at least $X"), so a client can show it beside `suggested_cap_usd` without parsing the
  sentence. It is carried from the refusal that wrote the words (a model request's and a paid call's alike), so the two never
  differ, and a pause serves it with its other figures, always.
- Sign in to a remote MCP server with OAuth, run by the server: `POST /v2/settings/mcp-servers/{name}/oauth/start` with `{url, scopes?, client_id?, client_secret?}` answers the `authorization_url` the owner opens, and `GET /v2/settings/mcp-servers/oauth/callback` (no token; a random state, used once, good for 10 minutes) exchanges the code, seals the token set under `AGENTRUN_SEAL_KEY` bound to the server's name, lists the tools and redirects to the console's MCP servers page. The official MCP SDK does discovery (RFC 9728, RFC 8414), PKCE S256 and the RFC 8707 resource; the client is the owner's pre-registered one, else the server's client metadata document (`GET .../oauth/client.json`, from a public https address only), else dynamic registration. Signing in again reuses the client kept from the last sign-in (a pre-registered client's id and secret are given once). Reads show `credential: sealed` and `oauth: {expires_at, scopes, client, client_id}`, never a token or client secret; `auth.type: "oauth"` is accepted on a `PUT`, which keeps the token set. Every launch of a run that grants the server's tools refreshes its token within 5 minutes of expiry (once at a time per server) and hands the run the access token alone; one that expires mid-run is refreshed at the next launch, and its calls meanwhile say to sign in again; a refused refresh reads `credential: "expired"` and asks to sign in again in Settings → MCP servers. Capabilities' `mcp_client.auth` gains `oauth`. The launcher's warning for a seal key it cannot use names MCP server credentials too and how to fix the file, `409 no_sealing_key` says how to get a seal key, and a plain-http URL off loopback is refused naming its host.
- OpenRouter's `particle-ai/particle-1` runs, is priced and can be capped: serve lists it beside pi-ai's catalog, with pi-ai 1.5.0's entry (1,048,576-token context, 131,072 output) at Particle's own list price, $0.25 a million input tokens, $0.03 cached input and $1.00 output, until the pi family moves to pi-ai 1.5. While OpenRouter's promotional price ($0.05, $0.003, $0.50) lasts, a run's reported cost on it over-states what OpenRouter bills: on pi-ai 1.1.0 the cost is computed from the catalog price, until the pi family reports the provider's billing (pi-ai 1.2 and later do; the family's move is to 1.5). A cap errs safe this way. A caller that records the cost from OpenRouter's own usage keeps the true figure.
- A workflow run takes a spend cap (`max_cost_usd`, `cap_mode`), on its start, from its agent's default (a workflow definition now keeps one), and from a trigger, which may now start a workflow. Each paid step is held to the cap before it is sent, as an agentic run's are: a model node's request, a call node's `llm` call and paid tool call, and a decision at its provider's price (Parallel's list price, Jev's published price, an OpenRouter Decisions model's catalog price). A step the cap has no room for waits unsent where it stands: the run is `input_required` with `pending.kind` budget and `pending.node`, and `PATCH /runs/{id}` sends it, in the same process or, after 10 minutes paused, in one opened again from the run's journal, with no completed node sent again. A capped start whose node runs on a model with no price here, or whose decision pins an OpenRouter Decisions model the catalog does not price, is refused before it starts (409 `agent_not_runnable`, the node and the fix in `error.fields`), as an agentic run's is. A decision whose price cannot be read mid-run (OpenRouter's catalog down) is read again, then fails unsent as a connection error (`DecisionProviderError("connection")`) and its decision node fails, decision nodes having no attempts loop: never as the cap's stop; a decision an earlier process asked and never saw answered holds nothing and never pauses for a raise; and a decision's cost counts on the next process from its receipt when its booking was lost, or, with no receipt that names its charge, at its hold, kept in the run's store once the driver admitted the request and right before it is sent, when its process died with the request maybe out; a request never admitted keeps no hold and is never counted.
  - `@parcha/agentrun-dsl`: `WorkflowDeps.held(executionPath)`, the time a host held a call, which its deadline does not count; `chargedCost` is exported.
  - `@parcha/agentrun-decisions`: the OpenRouter provider's `priceOf(model)`, the model's catalog price.
  - `@parcha/pi-durable-agent`: a workflow revision keeps `maxCostUsd` and `capMode`.
- A signing secret the server mints is shown once, as `signing_secret`, and only to the owner's session. A webhook trigger's secret (`POST /v2/triggers`, `POST /v2/triggers/{trigger_id}/secret`) and the outbound webhook's (the first `PUT /v2/settings/webhook`) are in the minting answer to the owner's signed-in session, or to the console the launch link opened on a loopback-bound server with accounts off. The API token gets the trigger with `secret_set: true` and, in `note`, "Open the console to see this trigger's signing secret once: rotate it there.", or the webhook with "Open the console to see this webhook's signing secret once: remove it and save it again there." Rotating a trigger's secret, and removing the outbound webhook (`DELETE /v2/settings/webhook`, its secret with it), are secret writes: the token is refused (403) in the secret-write words, the secret it would replace still signs and the webhook stays. Each answer and read carries the secret's metadata as `secret`, in the one shape (name, set, source, changed_at), never the value: a trigger's is named `trigger:<id>` (GET and the list too; `changed_at` is in the create's and rotation's answers, null on a read), the outbound webhook's `webhook:outbound`, its `changed_at` the time the secret was made, kept across a URL change, and null for a webhook set before this release. A template that makes a webhook trigger says so in its apply answer, a warning under `triggers.<i>.secret`. Upgrading: the shown-once value moved from `secret` to `signing_secret` in those three answers, and `secret` is now the metadata object; a script that created webhook triggers with the token gets no value at all: take the secret from the console's Triggers screen (rotate it there). A script that removed the outbound webhook with the token gets a 403: remove it in the console.
- Applying a template says beforehand when a GitHub tool it grants is one Connect GitHub's App can't use. The App asks for the least its templates need (issues: read), so release-notes' one write, `mcp:github/issue_write`, which needs issues: write, is refused by GitHub at every call. Where the github MCP server runs on the App's tokens, the apply answer now carries a warning under `grants.mcp:github/issue_write` (`not_allowed`, beside a tool the server denies): "mcp:github/issue_write needs Issues: write, and this server's GitHub App has Issues: read, so GitHub refuses it: release-notes keeps its draft in its answer. The App's owner can add Issues: write to it on GitHub." The console's Templates screen shows it with the apply's other warnings. The App's permissions are unchanged.
- A template applied from the owner's session shows each webhook trigger it made its signing secret once. `POST /v2/templates/{name}/apply` answers `webhook_secrets`, one per webhook trigger it made (absent when it made none): its `trigger_id`, its secret's metadata as `secret` (named `trigger:<id>`), and, in the first answer to the owner's signed-in session alone, `signing_secret`, as `POST /v2/triggers` shows it. The Idempotency-Key's record never keeps a value: a replay under the same key answers `secret_set` and a note that it was shown once, and an apply with the API token answers `secret_set` and the words that send the person to the console to rotate it there. The warning under `triggers.<i>.secret` stays. Upgrading: a script that read `webhook_secrets[].secret` as the value now reads `signing_secret`, from the owner's session; the token gets none.
- `agentrun template eval DIR` (`agentrun-serve eval-template DIR`) runs a template package's eval cases as real runs of its agent and grades each run, so a package's quality bar is measured. By default it runs on a server of its own in the command's process over a temp data directory, so it never touches yours; `--server URL` runs on that one, through its v2 API alone. Per case it serves each `mcp` fixture as a fake MCP server on loopback that records every call, applies the package with the case's parameters and grants (a fresh agent per run), seeds the case's learned notes, starts each run with the prompt and the case's cap, and grades the answer, the tool calls and the files: `schema`, `regex`, `tool_used` and `file_exists` deterministically, `llm` as a run of a blind grader agent on the same server (`--grader-model`). A case passes when every grader passes on every run. The summary goes to stdout and the whole report, with each run's and each grade's cost, to `--report FILE` as JSON; the exit is 0 when every case passed, 1 when one failed, 2 when nothing ran. Every case is read and checked before anything runs, and the tests check that every shipped package's cases load and their fixtures serve. `@modelcontextprotocol/server` (2.4.0, already a dev dependency) is now a dependency of `@parcha/agentrun-serve`, for the fake MCP servers: it brings no package the server did not already install.
- Template packages: one folder that makes a working agent, applied by one call. A package is `agent.md` (its YAML front matter is the whole manifest, its body the agent's instructions), `skills/<name>/SKILL.md` in the Agent Skills format, `rules/*.md` (shipped beside each skill's SKILL.md) and `evals/<case>/` in the plugin-eval layout (`prompt.md`, `graders/*.md`). The manifest names the package (`name`, the agent's `display_name`, a SemVer `version`, `description`, `license`, `author`, `repository`), the harnesses it prefers, its model as a tier with a preferred model, its `effort`, `tools`, the MCP servers and tools it uses and those it asks the owner to allow (`mcp: [{server, tools, required?, allow?}]`), typed `parameters`, the model or tool keys it needs by name (`secrets`), its `budget` (required with triggers), `learning`, `triggers` (each named), its `output` schema and `checks`. `GET /v2/templates` lists the packages the server ships, and `POST /v2/templates/{name}/apply` with `{parameters, grants?, overrides?: {model}}` makes the package's agent and its triggers through the same code as `POST /v2/agents` and `POST /v2/triggers`: parameters that do not check are a 422, a missing secret or required MCP server is `409 agent_not_runnable` with nothing made, an MCP server the package can do without is skipped with a warning, one the server has saved has the package's tools of it granted at the owner's own settings (a package sets no permission: only the apply's `grants`, the owner's answer, become allow, and never over a deny the owner set on the server), and a trigger the server refuses deletes the triggers made before it and archives the agent. The agent's model is the package's preferred one when the server runs it, else the server's model for the tier (fast `AGENT_MODEL_FAST`, balanced `AGENT_MODEL`, strong `AGENT_MODEL_STRONG`), and `overrides.model` wins. A package's version is never `definition.version`, the revision number. The server checks every package it ships at its start, whole (its agent and trigger bodies compiled and checked as those routes check them, at most 256 files and 1 MiB), and a broken one stops the start. The CLI gains `agentrun template list`, `agentrun template apply` and `agentrun template validate DIR`, which runs the server's own check on a folder.
  - The first package is `code-review`: a code review agent for one GitHub repository (`parameters.repo`), named `Code review: <repo>`, on pi, with the fast tier and `openrouter/particle-ai/particle-1` preferred, low effort, each run capped at $0.50, learning off. Its review skill proves each finding against the code and answers with findings JSON (`findings.schema.json`), and, when its tools are a GitHub MCP server's instead of a shell, posts one review with inline comments, never an approval. It uses six tools of a GitHub MCP server named `github` where the server has one saved, and none where it does not; it asks the owner to allow its four read tools, and its two review writes ask. It has no triggers yet.
- The server ships five template packages, none needing a sandbox: `code-review`, `kyb`, `market-monitor`, `inbound-lead` and `release-notes`. No package puts a write or a send in its `allow`: each one reads content someone else wrote, and a write asks until the owner grants it at apply.
  - **`kyb`**, business due diligence on one company:
    - identity from primary registries, owners and officers;
    - sanctions screened against the OFAC, UN, EU and UK lists through the list owners' own records, never a news article about them;
    - dated adverse media, red flags graded by the owner's risk appetite, and a verdict.
    Every claim is cited, and nothing is invented.
  - **`market-monitor`**, a weekly digest of what changed at the companies you watch: launches, pricing, hiring, funding and news. Each item is dated and linked, and an event counts only when it happened in the window. It sends to one Slack channel ID or one email address set at apply. Its schedule comes from its `cron` and `timezone` parameters, Monday 08:00 UTC by default.
  - **`inbound-lead`**, a webhook trigger for form or CRM submissions:
    - it researches the person and the company, and scores the lead against the owner's ICP with evidence for each criterion;
    - it writes back to HubSpot only onto the contact whose email matches the lead, or posts to one Slack channel ID.
    - each lead is its own run: the trigger takes Standard Webhooks bodies of type `lead.submitted` (`accept`), at most 3 at once, so a lead that arrives while another is researched is never skipped.
  - **`release-notes`** turns the PRs merged since the last release into user-facing notes and leaves them as a draft issue to edit. No MCP tool makes a draft release. It never publishes. With no GitHub server saved, it works from a list of PRs in the message.
  - **`code-review`** reads through the GitHub tools when it has no shell (never cloning), and reads its rules with `read`. It gains a `focus` parameter and six eval cases.

  A package parameter may have a `default`. A schedule's `cron` and `timezone` may come from parameters; such a parameter needs a default, and the package's own schedule is checked with it. Apply now checks every trigger, its parameters filled, before it makes anything: a bad cron or zone, a date that has passed, or a webhook trigger on a server with no seal key (`409`, `triggers.<i>`) refuses the apply with nothing made. Before, the trigger's create failed after the agent was made and archived it. A package's answer schema (`output.schema`) now ships beside each SKILL.md, as its rules do, so the agent can read the shape it must answer in. The templates README documents the eval cases, one fake MCP server fixture format, and how to scope a saved server's credential.
- A template package may say how its agent runs: `approvals: never` (unattended) or `ask` (the default). `GET /v2/templates` shows it as `approvals_default`, and an apply takes it when it sends no `overrides.approvals`. code-review, inbound-lead, market-monitor and release-notes run unattended, and kyb asks. Their writes are held by their pins (release-notes' draft issue is now pinned to `method: create`), or by a rule the template tests state. Until the agent store's cutover an agent keeps no pin of its own, so apply makes a pinned write `deny`, with a `pins.<tool>` warning, unless the server's own settings pin that tool; that server pin then holds the agent's calls, and removing it lifts the hold until the cutover keeps the agent's own pin. A write a template offers for the owner's grant is either pinned or bounded by a rule its tests state (code-review's pending-review comment adds only to the pinned review). An apply that makes a webhook trigger (inbound-lead's) hands back each one's signing secret once, in `webhook_secrets` of its first answer, as `POST /v2/triggers` does; a retry under the same Idempotency-Key gets a `note` in its place.
- Each tool has a `blurb`: one short line for people, such as web_fetch's "Read a public web page" or ask_person's "Ask you a question mid-run". It is served beside the model-facing `summary` in `capabilities.tools[]` and in `GET /v2/agents/{id}`'s `tools`, from one source, so both agree, and the model never sees it. An MCP server's tool has none. It is optional in the schema, so a console falls back to the summary on an older server.
