  Capabilities say it: `models.priced` (new) is false for such a default, and null with no default model. And a run with `max_cost_usd` on it is refused
  before it starts, 409 on `definition.model` in the cap's words, instead of being refused at its first request; an agent
  saved with a default cap on one gets that as a warning.
- A spend cap's wrap-up no longer tells a run to wrap up before it has done anything on a common cap. While nothing is priced, the typical request it is measured against is the next request at its input and a 4,096-token answer, not its whole output allowance: a $1 run on Claude Sonnet 4.5 was told at its start (its 64,000-token allowance alone is $0.96), and now is not. A note that comes due while nothing is priced says the budget ("This run's budget is about $X, enough for little work: answer as soon as you can.") on claude-agent, codex and opencode too, as on pi, never "finish now".
- A spend cap's wrap-up told before the run's own model has a priced request (a small cap on a pricey model, due at its first request) now says "This run's budget is about $X, enough for little work: answer as soon as you can." instead of asking it to finish before it has started. Later it says, as before, "About $X left of this run's budget: finish now with your answer." Both read from the same priced requests live and after a restart.
- An agent's own ask or allow no longer overrides a deny set on the server; the call is refused on this server. Where the server's own setting for a tool (`/v2/settings/tools`: its exact key, else its MCP server's wildcard `mcp:<server>/*`; bash's for a shell tool) is deny, that deny is absolute: it holds over any setting in an agent's `definition.tool_settings`, a template's grant among them, and whatever the agent's `approvals`. The agent read says `permission: "deny"`, `permission_from: "server"`. With no server deny, the agent's own setting still comes first. Upgrading: an agent that set a tool to ask or allow on a server that denies it now has that tool refused; lift the server's deny to restore it.
- The server's record (`.server.json` in its data directory) is written under a name of its own and renamed into place, so a reader that finds it (`npx @parcha/agentrun` reads it to find a running server) reads all of it, never an empty file the server has made but not filled yet.
- Setup at a public address takes the right setup code however the console was opened. `GET /v2/auth/session` says, before a password is set, whether setup takes the code the server printed (`setup_code: true` at a public address, `false` on an open setup, never the code itself), and the console's setup form shows its Setup code field whenever it does, with where to find the code ("Find the setup code in the server's log (on Render, the Logs tab), or open the link the log prints."), and sends no setup until a code is in it. The server's API token (AGENTRUN_TOKEN) works in that field too, compared as the Authorization header's is; a wrong one counts as a wrong code. A setup with no code is refused without counting against the printed code; the fifth wrong code voids it and prints a new one at once, and the refusal says a new one is in the log. A setup link opened in the same tab after the page loaded fills the field too. Before, a first visit at the plain address showed no field, its tries with no code voided the printed code without printing a new one, and the right code from the log was then refused as "not the setup code". At an https public address, the console's pages also carry Strict-Transport-Security (max-age one year); over http or on loopback with no public address, none.
- A run stopped mid-answer reads its cost as unknown plus what is known so far, with the stopped request as one charge with no price, from the moment it reads stopped. Before, until the turn's end reached the server from the run's process, it read the known sum as its cost (`cost.usd` the earlier turns' total, no `unpriced`). The served cost and the usage row only: the spend cap's reads (the run's store) and its bounds are unchanged.
- The server's tool settings saved to the one store keep a tool's pin, and a pin that does not read, exactly as the file's save does: a pin is a gate fence, and a save that sends the tool's key back without a pin of its own keeps a broken one refusing until a valid pin or the key left out clears it. Both saves now share one rule (keptSettings), so they cannot drift.
- A take-over now reads as it happened on the Timeline. "You took over the browser" and "You handed the browser back" each show the time it happened, so the gap between them is how long you held the browser. Before, each showed the time of the row above it, so an 11 s hold could read as 3 s. The agent's browser call that waited for your hand-back says so: "Waited for you 11 s, then opened www.example.com/…". Before, it read as the agent opening the page while you had the browser. Upgrading: nothing to do.
  - pi-browser: a browser call that waited for the person adds `waited_for_person_ms` to its result's `details`; one stopped while it waited adds nothing.
  - pi-durable-agent: a `HOST_PHASE` entry's `at` (ms) is its row's time, and a browser call's row whose result has `waited_for_person_ms` starts "Waited for you <time>, then …" when the call succeeded, and "Waited for you <time>: …" when it failed.
- A template package's trigger input is checked against the input its agent takes (`{content: text}`) when the package is read, so a package whose trigger every apply would refuse no longer passes `agentrun template validate` or a server's start. `GET /v2/templates`' `grants` text and the templates README say that a grant is the agent's own tool setting, which outranks a server-wide deny set after the apply. Tests now pin a server-wide `mcp:<server>/*` deny against grants, a saved key satisfying a package's `secrets`, and apply's 413. The code-review skill's event gate reads the event and action from the `<trigger-payload>` tag when it names them, else from the payload's `action` and shape. It doesn't stop on a field cut from a payload over 16 KiB when the trigger's filter checked that field, and it reads draft, fork and labels with `pull_request_read` after the gate.
- Template fixes from the human QA on the QA cut:
  - A template parameter can declare `enum`, a closed set of values that `GET /v2/templates` shows and apply checks (`not_an_option` outside it). `inbound-lead`'s `post_to` and `kyb`'s `risk_appetite` use it in place of a pattern, so the console shows a choice. Patterned parameters' descriptions now say their format in words ("a Slack channel ID like C0123ABCD").
  - `kyb` raises a registry status flag (a dissolution notice, overdue filings) only from the company's own status and filing fields, never from help text a registry shows on every record, and checks the record again before such a flag moves the verdict. Only the page's generic text is excluded: a dated notice or petition naming the company still applies. Corporations Canada is named among the registries, with that caveat, and a new eval case (an active Canadian corporation that has filed, its record captured with the hidden help text) holds it.
  - `market-monitor`'s digest names every watched company: one with nothing in the window gets its own line, "nothing new this week (checked: ...)", so a quiet company reads differently from a skipped one. A new eval case with two companies holds it.
- Template follow-ups after the QA cut:
  - The template eval runner grades a background shell call (`bash_background`, `bash_output`, `bash_list`, ...) under its own name, as the tool gate names its request; it was counted as `bash`, so its own `tool_used` grader never saw it.
  - `kyb` has an overflow rule for beneficial owners: past 10 (a public company's holders of 5% or more) it keeps the largest holdings and says in `limitations` how many it left out, citing the filings that list them.
- Template fixes from QA on Render:
  - On a gateway server, a package with only a model tier applied with `422 definition.model is "provider/model-id"`: the tier's model was the gateway's own id. That model is now left out, so the agent runs on the server's default.
  - On a gateway server, a package's preferred model is no longer picked, since the gateway never said it serves it. Apply says so in a `model` warning; `overrides.model` picks one the gateway serves.
  - `GET /v2/templates` shows each package's `author` and its answer's schema (`output: {schema, json_schema}`).
  - `kyb`'s answer fits its output limit: the report is a short narrative, never a copy of the structured fields.
  - `inbound-lead`'s score is computed from the criteria it lists, by its rubric, so a missed must or a met disqualifier always caps it.
  - `market-monitor` reads small slices of pages (at most a few per company), so its context stays small.
  - Template budgets are raised so they never stop real work: `per_run_usd` 5 for kyb, market-monitor, inbound-lead and release-notes, 2 for code-review.
  - `kyb` can also look names up in OpenSanctions, one index of every sanctions list: an optional `opensanctions` parameter, off by default. Its description states the licence (CC BY-NC 4.0: non-commercial use; commercial use needs a paid OpenSanctions licence), and a run that uses it says so in `limitations`. OpenSanctions is an index: the list owner's record stays the evidence.
- Template fixes from human QA:
  - `code-review` and `release-notes` refuse a repository with a trailing dot (`acme/widgets.`): `repo` is owner/name ending in a letter, digit, `-` or `_`.
  - `code-review` reads no repo file when it reviews a pasted diff with no repository behind it, instead of failing reads of `REVIEW.md`, `AGENTS.md`, `.pr_agent.toml`, `.git/` and the changed files; it says the repo's own rules were not read.
- A webhook trigger's secret is cut from every Timeline row, as a saved key is: by its value in each of its forms (hex, base64, JSON-escaped), and its bare signing key (the base64 after `whsec_`), not only by its `whsec_` shape, and from a run's title and answer too. After a rotation the new secret is cut at once. Upgrading: nothing to do.
- The trigger sources, the schedule and the signed webhook, are adapters in one registry, and every list of sources is read from it: the source kinds a create takes and its check, a firing's `by` and key, the payload's tag, each run's provenance line, `GET /v2/capabilities` and the OpenAPI document's source schemas. Nothing a client sees changes, but the OpenAPI document's firing `by` now says what `manual` is. Upgrading: nothing to do.
- A schedule whose `every_s` a hand edit of its store row put out of range (below 60 seconds, or past a year and a day) is turned off with `disabled: {reason: "source_unreadable"}` and a log line, as a cron line or zone that no longer reads is, instead of firing on a step no request could set.

  Archiving an agent turns off every trigger that runs as it (a `start_run` of it, a `message_run` to one of its runs) in the archive's own store transaction, with `disabled.reason` `agent_archived`, and the archive's answer lists them in `triggers_off`. Unarchiving turns none on: its answer lists those still off. While the agent is archived, a trigger made on, turned on or aimed at it is `409 agent_archived`, and a firing that comes due anyway is skipped ("The agent <name> is archived.") and starts no run: the store refuses a run of an archived agent in the transaction that would start it.

  A webhook trigger's view (create, GET, the list, PATCH) carries `hook_url`, where its sender posts: its own `/v2/hooks/{id}`, at the server's public URL when it has one, else the path. A schedule has none.

  A webhook trigger that starts runs takes `max_active_runs` with or without `accept`. Set, each event starts its own run up to the cap; past it the firing waits due, and past 20 due an event answers `429 rate_limited` with `Retry-After`. Without a cap, a trigger with no `accept` keeps the overlap skip as before. A schedule keeps the overlap skip and still refuses `max_active_runs`, as does a `message_run` target, which starts no run.
- A trigger's input, a started run's first message or a firing's message to a run, enters the run as the trigger's, never as the person's: its user entry carries the origin `{kind: "trigger", trigger_id, source, owner}` (`messageOrigins` reads it), with `owner` set by the trigger's definition as it fires and never by its payload (true for a schedule, on time or by hand; false for a webhook, its body empty or not). The model reads the very text it did; the run's timeline shows a `phase` row, "Trigger <its id>: <its first line>", with `origin: "trigger"`, never "You: ...". In `@parcha/pi-durable-agent`, `MessageOrigin` gains the trigger kind and `originOf` reads it (`TRIGGER_SOURCES`), and a `MESSAGE_ORIGIN` marker with `withdrawn: true` takes back an open one. In `@parcha/pi-durable-disk`, a run's door takes a trigger origin, on an input that may not answer a person (`answers: false`) too, and withdraws its marker when that input is refused busy. Upgrading: nothing to do.
- A trigger's message to a run that waits on its person (a tool call parked for its owner's approval, a question, a pause at its cap) is refused and its firing skipped, "The run is waiting on a person.", even while another message to the run is in flight: it never declines the approval, nor answers. A person's own message still declines a parked call, as before. Upgrading: nothing to do.
- Every pushed trigger source is served by one handler at `POST /v2/hooks/{trigger_id}` (a source whose settings are a trigger's, the webhook) or `POST /v2/hooks/{source}` (one whose settings are the server's), with no token, in one order: headers, the body cut at the source's limit (413 at once, as declared or as it streams, and the socket closed without reading the rest; 408 when the whole body takes over 30 s), its check against its settings, its handshake, its parse. Every refusal is the one 401. A request that passes its check but is no event its source takes, or names no trigger of it, is answered 200 and said in the log (`hook_unparsed`, `hook_no_trigger`), never swallowed; a hook path that does not decode is said as `malformed_path`. A scope belongs to one trigger: a trigger of a server-wide source on a scope another enabled one of its kind listens on is `409 scope_taken` (`details: {source, scope, trigger_id}`), at a create, an enabling or a move, and an event whose scope more than one trigger listens on (a file edited by hand) fires none and is said (`hook_scope_ambiguous`). Every request's headers must arrive within 10 s. The webhook trigger answers as before. Upgrading: nothing to do.
- A trigger's Timeline row names its source and its trigger: "Schedule utc-check: Tell me the time in UTC." where it read "Trigger trg_…: …". pi-durable-agent labels the row by its source (`TRIGGER_LABELS`: Schedule, Webhook, Slack, GitHub) and keeps `trigger: {id, source, owner}`; serve names the trigger as it is now on every page, the stream and the final rows, and by its id once the trigger is deleted. A trigger renamed renames its runs' rows: the name it had when it fired is not kept yet, and comes when the message that fires a run carries its trigger's name. A run a trigger starts is titled from its trigger, "Webhook inbound-lead" (and the event its payload's tag names, "Webhook reviews: pull_request opened"), where it read its input, a sender's `<trigger-payload>` markup among it.
- A trigger's runs on the store read as main's did. A trigger's Timeline row names the trigger by its name as it is now, read by its id at each read (a rename shows at once; a deleted trigger is named by its id; a held value in the name is cut), and a trigger's run is titled from its trigger, never from its input. A trigger's message to a run is queued as a command row with its firing marked sent in one write, within the trigger's bound per run, so a server that goes down after it never sends it twice, and a run that has ended takes none. `GET /v2/triggers` no longer documents a triggers.json that does not read: a store busy with another write answers 409 home_busy.
- The server keeps one list of its own secrets (its tokens, its outbound webhook's secret, and every value a trigger source keeps sealed), and what a run shows (its answer, its Timeline, its errors), what learning keeps and what an import writes are all cut of it: a webhook trigger's signing secret, and the outbound webhook's, were missing from what a run shows. A signing secret is cut in every spelling (whole, its key as base64 padded or not, base64url and hex, and with its / escaped). A trigger's secret that is rotated or removed, and the outbound webhook's that is replaced or deleted, stays cut while the server runs: it is kept in memory only, so an old secret pasted after a restart is shown, when it signs nothing any more. A webhook trigger's secret that does not open under the seal key the server starts with is now said in its log (`condition=trigger_setting_unopened`), where it was dropped in silence. The inbound and the outbound webhook share one Standard Webhooks module (signatures unchanged, byte for byte). A trigger source's sealed settings live in `DIR/trigger-settings-<source>.json`. Upgrading: webhook triggers made before this release lose their secret (it was kept in `trigger-secrets.json`, which is no longer read, and which the server's start says it found, `condition=trigger_secrets_left_over`): rotate it with `POST /v2/triggers/{id}/secret`, or make the trigger again.
- Packages take TypeBox's `Type`, `Static` and `TSchema` from typebox, never through pi-ai, pi-agent-core, pi-durable or chord, which drop those re-exports from pi 1.5. serve, pi-browser and pi-agent-learn now depend on typebox 1.3.27 directly (the version pi-ai 1.1 resolves); harness and agentrun-pi keep it as a host-provided peer. Tool schemas serialize exactly as before, so tool arguments are checked the same. harness's `shownShots` has a named return type, `ShownShots`.
- A cloud phone starts on a server with the default box allow-list. A computer whose provider runs only on the vendor's own network (a cloud phone, a hosted browser: its declared network is `open` alone) now asks for `open` whatever the run's allow-list says, instead of the list it refused at create; this server's allow-list does not reach a network the vendor runs.
- A run's bash call in a hosted VM sandbox no longer fails with "could not close the program's input: Error: no such program" on a loaded machine. The box relay let a program go once its exit and its output were read, and the program's input close, sent separately, could land after that and get "no such program". The relay now remembers the last 4096 programs it let go after their exit. A late call for one of them is answered as already ended: an input close or write succeeds and does nothing, and an exit read gives the same exit code. A read of its output fails (410), since that output is no longer held. A program id the relay never had, or one of another run, still gets 404.
- On the runner, the web tools read the Parallel key from each call, and no client, key or fetched page outlives its run: one run's key or pages never serve another's. A run's monid price quotes are its own too.
- Upgrading: a webhook outbox written by a server before the ledger (v2) is not read; nothing to do.
- A workflow's model node submits on the runner. Every model request on the runner goes through the session's model proxy, whose policy drops each tool definition it is not given, and a workflow's proxy was given none: a node's request reached its model with no `submit`, so no workflow could finish there. The workflow's proxy now admits the node's whole offered set by name, its `submit` and the tools its workflow's model nodes name, on Anthropic's Messages and OpenAI's Responses alike, as a pi session's proxy admits its own tools. agentrun-pi exports the tool's name as `SUBMIT_TOOL`.
- A capped run's wrap-up note is carried by the next request when the request it was given never leaves. On another harness, that happens when a stop refuses the request after its admission (a park, a halt, the cancel); the first request after a park, the resumed turn's first, went without the note, and only the one after it carried it. On pi, it happens when the request's send throws as it is made: the note stayed pending on a request that never existed, and the run was never told.
- A capped run's wrap-up note counts as told only once a request carrying it is answered, on every harness. A request the provider never answers, one lost on the way, one cut, one stopped part way, or one that never leaves, leaves the note for the next request, which carries it. Before, the note counted as told as soon as a request carrying it was admitted, so a retry after a failed request went without it, and the run could reach its cap unwarned. On another harness, a note told in an answered request is never told again in a later process, even if the row that shows it was lost: the row is rebuilt from the answered request's own record. On pi, the run's wrap-up row, written once the carrying request is answered, is what a later process reads.

  Upgrading: a host that implements the kernel's `ModelPort` may return `note` in a request's admission (written on its `runner.turn` record when it ends) and read `ended` on `request_end`; both are optional.
- An attached Workspace's test of a write's call order no longer depends on the umask of whoever runs it: it sets its files' modes itself, and checks both a file that gets the write setting its mode and a private one that needs none.
- `barrier-box-loss.test.mjs`'s workflow case kills the run once the effect's line is in its file, not once the file exists: the effect opens the file and then writes its line, so a kill between the two left it empty and the case failed with `'' !== 'first\n'` (seen on CI run 42435209304).
- A claude-agent run whose process is SIGKILLed while its Claude CLI has a model request out leaves nothing running: a test now holds that every process under the killed one, the CLI among them, is gone within 2 s of the kill, that the CLI's model proxy refuses connections, that no request reaches the provider from the killed process, and that the run completes on its next process.
- A serve test's server started on another server's data (a restart's, `serve(t, { data: first.data })`) is closed before the first server's after-hook removes that directory. node:test runs after-hooks in the order they were added, so the first server's ran first and removed the directory while the second, and its run's process, could still write there (`ENOTEMPTY`); the hook's failure skipped the second's close, so the file then hung until its timeout. `support.mjs` `serve()` now closes every server started on a directory it made before it removes it, and `trigger-engine.test.mjs`'s `engine()` does the same for its engines. `learned-route-real.test.mjs`'s restart cases also close their second server themselves and check that no process of their runs outlives them, as its cut case does.

## 0.1.0-beta.31 (2026-11-06)

- `bash_background` takes `tty: true` to run its command on a terminal: `script -qfec` in the box gives it a pty, so a program that needs one (a prompt, a REPL, a progress display) works in the background.
  - Paging, input and signals are the same calls. The log holds what the terminal shows, with input echoed and lines ending in `\r\n`. `bash_input` types into the terminal. `bash_signal` reaches the terminal's processes, and `INT` goes to its foreground job, as Ctrl-C does.
  - A box with no `script` (util-linux) refuses `tty: true` in words and starts nothing. `bash_list` marks a terminal process `(tty)`.
  - A terminal process is its terminal's session: a child its command leaves running on the terminal keeps it running, and signals and the run's end reach that child after `script` itself is gone.
- A browser charge now shows on the browser call's own row, not on a "Tool cost" row of its own. Before, every browser call added a row like "Tool cost: browserbase charged $0.0002 for browser", and the session's release added another, all for fractions of a cent that Usage already counts.
  - **The call's row carries it.** The browser call's `tool_use` row has `usage: {"usd": 0.0017, "requests": 0}`: every charge booked under that call, summed. Once one of them has no price, it reads `{"usd": null, "requests": 0, "so_far_usd": ..., "unpriced": ...}`. A browser charge makes no row of its own.
  - **A release lands on the last call.** The release's charge is booked under the session's last call, so it lands on that call's row. A charge that names no call (a session a reopened process released) goes to the last call charged for its session, else to its conversation's latest browser call.
  - **Live, it is an update.** A charge booked after the answer is served on `/v2` as a change of that row, with the same `id` and the new `usage`. The live reducer re-emits the row.
  - **Totals do not change.** The run's cost, its usage row ("... and 3 paid tool calls") and `/usage` count each charge once, as before. On a live run, `/usage` reads `browser_time` from these rows; for an ended run it reads the store, as before.
  - **Other tools' charges are unchanged.** monid's and Parallel's web_search keep their own "Tool cost" row.
  - **pi-durable-agent** exports `BROWSER_CHARGE` and `chargedTool(row)`. `chargedTool` names the tool whose charge a row carries. The text view shows a call's charge on its line ("Read the page "Example" · $0.0017").
  - **OpenAPI** notes on `TimelineRow.usage` where it shows here. The shape stays the contract's `TimelineUsage`.
- `npx @parcha/agentrun` manages agents and runs from the terminal: `agents list|get|create|update`, `runs start|get|list|message|stop|cancel|raise-cap`, `triggers ...`, `usage`, `stats` and a command for every other operation the server's OpenAPI document offers, plus `api METHOD PATH [--header 'NAME: VALUE']` for any route. An operation the server turns off has no command, and neither has `learning approve`, which takes the owner's password session in the console and refuses every token. `npx @parcha/agentrun help` lists the commands and `<command> --help` gives a command's arguments. Path parameters are positional, each encoded whole (`learning read AGENT skills/find-policies/SKILL.md`) except a file's path, which keeps its slashes (`home read AGENT notes/a.md`); query parameters, header parameters (`--idempotency-key`, `--if-match`) and a JSON body's top-level scalar fields are flags (`--max-cost-usd 2`); `--body JSON|@FILE|-` gives the whole body, its fields under the flags, and `--body-type TYPE` sends a body of another type the route takes as it is (`files create --path notes.txt --body @notes.txt --body-type text/plain`). A JSON body with no flags goes byte for byte. The response's JSON goes to stdout (indented on a terminal), a file's bytes exactly as the server sent them, and an error's JSON to stderr; exit codes are 0 ok, 1 the server refused, 2 bad usage, 3 the server cannot be reached, 4 no token or the token refused. A command whose body carries a secret (`tool-keys save`, `mcp-servers save|preview`, and `api` on any path that leads to one) takes its body only from stdin or a file.
  The server and token come from `--base-url`, else `AGENTRUN_BASE_URL` and `AGENTRUN_API_KEY`, else `base_url` and `api_key` in `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/agentrun/config.json`, else the server started on this machine with the same `--data` (default `~/.agentrun`). The token is never a flag and never printed, a key that is not a valid header value is refused before any request, and the local server's token goes to that server only. The local token is read from `DIR/.token` alone, and the config file, `DIR/.server.json` and `DIR/.token` are used only when they are regular files of the user's own with no group or other permission (exit 4 otherwise). `api` refuses a PATH with a `.` or `..` segment, plain or encoded, or an empty one. A reader that closes stdout early (`| head`) ends the command quietly. With no positional argument the command still starts the server, and `import` and `reset-password` are still the server's.
  `agentrun-serve` writes `DIR/.server.json` (`{base_url, pid, token_file}`, mode 0600, never the token; `token_file` is absent when the token came from `AGENTRUN_TOKEN`) once it listens and removes it on a clean exit; a file whose server is gone is reported as such, exit 3. The command table is generated from the server's document by `node scripts/generate-clients.mjs`, with names in `scripts/clients/resources.json`; its header hashes only what the table is made of, and `--check` fails when the committed table differs.
- The Docker box is on by default. A server started with `AGENTRUN_BOXES` not set at all (a plain `npx @parcha/agentrun`) probes Docker at its start, as if `docker` were listed, and offers it when Docker answers, so a new agent lands in the Docker box with a shell. Where Docker does not answer, new agents run on the runner, and the banner (`Boxes: none, so new agents run on the runner. Docker isn't running on this machine (no docker command on PATH): install or start Docker, then restart AgentRun.`), `capabilities.places.default_reason`, docker's `reason` and the console's new-agent form say why. With Docker on, the banner says `Boxes: docker (on by default; set AGENTRUN_BOXES to change).`
  An `AGENTRUN_BOXES` you set still wins: a list probes only what it names, and an empty value or `none` sets up no box (`none` stands alone). Daytona, the VM box and bwrap stay yours to list. Docker's reasons say what is wrong in plain words first (`Docker isn't running on this machine`), then what to do.
  The box image's first build is said in the terminal as it starts (`Preparing the Docker box image (first start only, a few minutes); agents can be made now, and their first run waits for it.`) and as it ends (`The Docker box image is ready.`). `npx @parcha/agentrun` now prints the `Boxes:` line and these two on stderr, after its `AgentRun is running at` line, so stdout stays that one line.
  `@parcha/pi-transport`, where the boxes' providers live, is now a dependency of `@parcha/agentrun-serve` rather than an optional peer, so a plain install (`npx @parcha/agentrun` included) has the Docker box.
- Each note, skill and workflow draft in the agent's learning view says its last change, `last_change: {kind, by, at}`: `approved`, `edited`, `created`, `retired` or `restored` by a `person`, or `learned` by a `run`, from the newest commit on its file. A retired note, skill or workflow draft can be read and brought back. The agent's learning view says of each retired one when and by whom it was retired (`retired: {at, by}`, `by` a `person` or a `run`), and its file's GET serves its whole text. `POST /v2/agents/{id}/learning/files/{file}/restore` makes it active again for the next run, in one commit on main, its owner's password session alone as an approval is; a draft is restored by its README.md. A restore checks the text again as a new note or skill is (a secret's shape, the host's own values, a hidden character, its format), and every file of a skill's or a draft's folder, so a value a retire took out of every run never comes back through it. A restored note marks the notes it supersedes, as an approval does, and an item that waited for its owner's OK before its retire is recorded as their approval (`Approved-Via: console`). `retired.at` is the time of the item's retire, which an edit since moves not.
- On a Mac whose Docker runs in a VM (Docker Desktop, OrbStack or colima), a Docker box reaches the server's host list too, through one proxy container inside the VM. Linux keeps its own path, unchanged.
  - The server runs the kernel's egress proxy in one container per server, `agentrun-egress-<12 hex>`, labelled `agentrun.egress`: `node:22-bookworm-slim` pinned by digest, the proxy's code bound read-only from the data root, the list as its one variable, a read-only root, no capability, the server's uid:gid. It listens on a Unix socket in a tmpfs volume owned by the server's uid, which the box mounts read-only at `/run/agentrun/egress/`. The box keeps `--network none`; the proxy container is the only container with a network, and it publishes no port.
  - It turns on only when `docker info` names a VM runtime the server knows (`Docker Desktop`, `OrbStack`, or a colima host name), on a kernel that is not the Mac's. Anything else stays offline, with `network_reason` saying what was seen.
  - At start the server sweeps the containers of its own label, pulls the image once if needed, starts the proxy and waits up to 10 s for it; until it is ready, or if it never is, the box's network is `none` with why. Docker restarts a proxy that dies, the server starts one that is gone again, and the server's stop removes it.
  - pi-transport's `dockerBoxes` takes `volumes: [{ name, target, readOnly }]`, named volumes every box mounts with `--mount type=volume`.
- An agent that learns says how many of its learned items wait for its owner's OK: `GET /v2/agents` and `GET /v2/agents/{agent_id}` carry `learning: { pending }` (notes, skills and workflow drafts held with `status: pending` on its learning folder's main), read once per commit of main, never per list call. Absent for an agent that does not learn, and on a server whose learning is off.
- MCP tools pass the run's one tool gate and its one spend cap. Each call enters the gate as `mcp:<server>/<tool>` (`mcp:<server>/*` sets all of a server's tools): a denied call is refused and nothing is sent; one set to ask, the default for MCP tools, parks and ends the turn on pi and claude-agent alike, and the owner's allow runs it once and resumes the run with its result. `PUT /v2/settings/mcp-servers/{name}` takes `prices` (USD a call, per listed tool): a priced call is held at its price under `max_cost_usd`, refused unsent when the cap cannot take it, and booked as the run's tool cost; an unpriced call runs "not metered", a timeline row and the usage row's `not_metered`, and `GET /usage`'s `tools` line counts them (`not_metered_calls`).
- A known MCP meta tool, one that runs other actions by name (Composio's `COMPOSIO_MULTI_EXECUTE_TOOL` and workbench, Klavis Strata's `execute_action`, Zapier's execute and `write_code_action`, Arcade's `UseTool`, Docker's `mcp-exec` and code mode, Cloudflare's `portal_execute` and code mode), is not grantable: a grant of it can't name the real action. A server's tools read it `grantable: false` with the reason, a save that grants it is `422`, and a run that pinned one is refused at its launch. Upgrading: an agent that grants one must grant the server's direct tools instead.
- Agents call a saved MCP server's tools on pi and claude-agent: grant `mcp:<server>/<tool>` in `definition.tools`. A save checks the name against the server's saved tools (`422` naming them). A run's first launch pins the granted tools as saved and the server's URL; its credential reaches only runs that grant one of its tools, over the run's secrets pipe. pi binds each tool in the run's process; claude-agent's own MCP client calls it at the session's model proxy (`/mcp/<server>`, behind the session key), so the CLI never holds the credential. A call follows no redirect, a crash-cut call is answered interrupted and never sent again, a moved server is sent nothing, and the timeline reads `<server> · <tool>`. `features.mcp_client.harnesses` lists the harnesses that run them. Every call passes the run's tool gate and its spend cap.
- **Under `bash: ask`, only the background calls that start or feed a process ask: `bash_output`, `bash_list` and `bash_signal` run unasked**
  - With `bash` set to `ask`, `bash_background` and `bash_input` (and `bash`) still park the run for the owner's approval, each under its own name. `bash_output`, `bash_list` and `bash_signal` now run without asking, so watching one approved process no longer costs an approval per page. They reach only the processes the run itself started, by its own ids: another run's id, or one never started, is answered "there is no background process <id> in this run".
  - `bash: deny` still refuses all five, and `bash: allow` runs them. None is a settings key of its own, so no setting of one lifts `bash`'s.
  - `capabilities.tools` and `GET /v2/agents/{id}`'s `tools` show the split: under `bash: ask`, the three watchers read `allow`, with `permission_from` where `bash`'s setting came from.
  - On a server with accounts off, the three watchers run under `bash: ask`, where an asked call is refused.
  - Only an `ask` set as written lets them run unasked. Where `bash` asks as a stand-in (its setting is a value not offered, or the settings file never read), they ask too. An agent's own setting that is no permission at all now reads `ask` in `capabilities` and its tools, as it already did at the gate, never the value as written.
  - That stand-in survives a save: `PUT /v2/settings/tools` with the map as `GET` served it (as the console saves every change) keeps it a stand-in and leaves the value as written in `tool-settings.json`, so neither the save nor a restart lets the watchers run unasked. The same `ask` sent again counts as no choice, since on the wire it can't be told from the stand-in; a different permission is a choice and is written as given.
- A model granted both web_fetch and the browser is now told which one has cookies. Asked to "Open https://httpbin.org/cookies", a pi run picked web_fetch, which has no cookies, and answered with an empty page; it picked the browser only when told to. The tool descriptions the model reads now say:
  - **web_fetch** has no cookies, sign-ins or session: it reads one page cold, so a page behind a sign-in, or one that depends on an earlier page, needs the browser tool, where this run has it. That holds for serve's web_fetch on every harness and for pi-browser's.
  - **The browser's `run`** keeps this agent's saved sign-ins and cookies, for anything behind a sign-in or a page that depends on an earlier one. It says this only on a run that has a saved sign-in: in serve, a run handed its agent's sign-in; in pi-browser, a host that passes `signIns: true` or a session vault. Without one it says only that the browser keeps cookies across pages, which stays true for a local Chrome, which keeps no saved sign-in.
  - **pi-browser** adds `browserToolsFor(signIns)` (the browser's contracts with `run`'s words for that plane), `BROWSER_KEEPS` (the two sentences), and the durable extension's `signIns` option.
- A trigger's message never reaches a run parked on a tool call's approval. The door's `POST /submit` with `answers: false` takes `parks` (entry kinds): it is refused with `409 WAITING`, by pi's root and by a door hook alike, while a call waits in a park of one of those kinds (an entry `{phase: "requested", id}` with no `{phase: "resolved", id}` after it). Serve sends its tool gate's park with each message a trigger sends, so a run that comes to wait on an approval just before the message reaches its door has the firing skipped ("The run is waiting on a person."), instead of taking the message, or being retried every 5 s as busy, and the approval stays pending. The park is read in one transaction (a hook's in the commit that admits the input), and only for a new admission: a requestId the door already took answers its record, so a retry of a delivered message settles sent.
- The `learned_workflow` tool shows each workflow's input as its schema says it, on a line under the workflow: its fields, their types, which are required (`*`) and a short description of each, so a call gives the input the workflow takes the first time. The line counts in the tool's description budget. A learned workflow draft whose output names no `path` is refused when it is kept and before a call runs any node: a learned workflow answers the value at `output.path`.
- A run that learns no longer keeps what the server tells it about learning, in a note or a skill, or a note beside a skill it keeps, which a grader could judge as planted and so drop the skill with it. A person's own instruction about learning is still theirs to keep. The run says it will keep what the person asked at the start of its reply, never after a sign-off they asked it to end with. When the grader keeps nothing, the run's learning names first the skill and the workflow draft it proposed, cut of the server's secrets each time it is read. A grader's answer the server cannot read is asked once more; when the second can't be read either, the run's learning is dropped and keeps that answer on the run's file, cut of the server's secrets and then to 2,000 characters, and no read serves it. Upgrading: nothing to do.
- A server on 127.0.0.1 answers `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` and `[::1]` on any port, so `ssh -L 8080:localhost:7340`, a proxy in front, or a start that took the next free port reach it. Before, the `Host` had to carry the server's own port, and anything else got `403 unexpected Host header`. The `AGENTRUN_PUBLIC_URL` name and each `--allow-host` name are served the same way, with or without a port, so an HTTPS proxy on the same machine that passes its own `Host` (Caddy's default) works. Every other name is still refused (the guard against DNS rebinding). A browser now gets a page that says which names work and how to add one; a script gets the same in the error's message.
- A key a tool needs and this server doesn't hold is named where a person fixes it, Settings first: "The tool monid.discover needs a Monid key: save one in Settings → Tool keys (or set MONID_API_KEY where the server starts)." It used to say only that the server's environment did not set MONID_API_KEY, which sent a person who had saved and removed the key in Settings to the wrong place. The same words cover every key Settings saves (Parallel, Monid, Browserbase, Kernel, TypeSafe (Jev), and the model keys): the save's warning, the run's 409, the tools view, capabilities' `reason` and `why` (a vendor picked without its key included), web_fetch's and web_search's answers, and a workflow's decision providers. A variable no key saves (AGENT_MODEL_API_KEY with AGENT_MODEL_BASE_URL) still names only where the server starts. The structured fields (`missing`, `host`) and the plane's stored values are unchanged.
- A VM box program whose start was still waiting on the provider when its run ended is now let go as soon as it starts. The server no longer reads its output or keeps it for ten minutes, and the run's process gets a 410 saying the run ended while the program started.
- The VM box tests' fake never reuses a sandbox id or directory within a process, so a server one test left still finishing its runs' ends cannot reach the next test's sandbox. The cancel test also checks that the turn's end copied the box's file back before it cancels, which shows which copy back is missing if it fails again.

## 0.1.0-beta.30 (2026-11-05)

- An agent can carry a default spend cap. A definition's `max_cost_usd` (agentic only) above 0 sets the agent's default, kept with its revision, as `maxCostUsd` on the agent store's agentic body, and read back in `GET /v2/agents/{agent_id}`; a run started with no `max_cost_usd` of its own takes its revision's default, and a run's own cap wins. An edit that leaves the field out keeps the agent's default, so leaving a field out never removes a spend limit, and only `max_cost_usd: 0` clears it (no default; through MCP's agents_revise too, where null is refused). A workflow definition with one above 0 is `422 not_allowed`, since a workflow run's spend is not capped here; 0 changes nothing there. A trigger's `start_run` target may now leave out `max_cost_usd` when its agent has a default: each firing takes the default as it stands when it starts, a target with neither is `422`, and a firing that finds neither fails rather than start an uncapped run.
- Background processes in a sandbox: an agent whose place is a sandbox may name `bash_background`, `bash_output`, `bash_input`, `bash_signal` and `bash_list`, to start a command that outlives one call (a dev server, a watcher, a long build), read its output in pages, write to its stdin, signal it and list what runs.
  - `bash_background {command}` answers the process's id at once. Its stdout and stderr go to `work/background/<id>/output.log`, beside its `pid` and, once it ends, its `exit` code, so the run's files show each log during the run and after it.
  - `bash_output {id, offset?, max_bytes?}` reads the log from a byte offset, at most 48 KiB a call, with whether the process still runs, its exit code, the log's size and the next offset. `bash_input {id, text, close?}` writes its stdin; `bash_signal {id, signal}` sends its process group `TERM`, `INT` or `KILL`.
  - At most 8 run at once in a run's box, and each log keeps the first 16 MiB of the output: a ninth start is refused in words.
  - The run's end ends every one of them (`TERM`, then `KILL` after 3 s) before its box is copied back and destroyed, and each log stays in the run's files.
  - Each call is one short command in the box, on the same path as `bash`, so they work on every box that runs `bash`, and the permission gate takes each call as it takes `bash`'s.
- Browser minutes count in a run's spend. Each charge of a `browser` session's time is booked as a tool cost, so it is a `Tool cost` line on the run's timeline, part of its `cost.usd`, and `/usage`'s new `browser_time` meter (dollars, and seconds for runs that have ended); capabilities list `browser_time` in `usage.meters`. Under `max_cost_usd`, the browser's time is held before it runs: a browser call that the cap cannot hold is not made, the model gets the cap's words, and the run's session is released, so no browser minute passes the cap.
  - Prices by provider: Browserbase $0.12 an hour with a 60 s minimum, Kernel (headless) about $0.06 an hour by the second, a local Chrome $0. `AGENTRUN_BROWSER_USD_PER_HOUR_<PROVIDER>` (`_BROWSERBASE`, `_KERNEL`, `_CDP`) sets a provider's own price in US dollars an hour. A provider with no price books its time as an unknown cost, and under a cap its calls are refused.
  - Each browser call is held at what its session ran since its last charge plus two stretches of session time; a stretch is the provider's minimum, pi-browser's 180 s idle window and 30 s for a release to be confirmed (270 s, $0.009, at Browserbase). A session keeps one stretch held until its release is booked.
  - pi-browser: `createBrowserExtension` takes `onCharge(charge, tx, call?)`, called with each session charge (`SessionCharge`: `seconds`, `usd`, `final`, `tag`, `provider`, `state`) in the commit that writes it on the session's record, at each browser call and at the release, or when custody gives up releasing a session. A session found by its tag (its create's answer lost) is billed at its release. A session kept across a reopen has its idle release armed at reconcile, so one no call uses is released an idle window later.
- Pick the browser: the `browser` tool runs on Browserbase or on Kernel, picked for the server (`PUT /v2/settings/tools` `{browser: {provider}}`) or for one agent (`definition.tool_settings.browser.provider`, which wins), as `web_search`'s provider is.
  - The choices are `auto` (the default), `browserbase`, `kernel` and `chrome`. `auto` is Browserbase where the server holds `BROWSERBASE_API_KEY` and `BROWSERBASE_PROJECT_ID`, else Kernel where it holds `KERNEL_API_KEY`, else unavailable, saying which keys would do. A vendor picked without its key is unavailable, never the other vendor. `chrome` is in the list but not set up on any server yet: it reads unavailable (`a local Chrome is not set up on this server`), and a run that would get it is `409 agent_not_runnable`.
  - A run keeps the vendor its first launch resolved for its life (its tool plane names it, `browser: "kernel"` or `"browserbase"`); at a later launch a vendor whose key is gone since is unavailable, and each browser call says why. Kernel's browsers run headless.
  - Kernel's key is a tool key: `PUT /v2/settings/tool-keys/kernel` `{values: {KERNEL_API_KEY}}` saves it sealed, after one check that costs nothing (Kernel's list of browsers, at most one, 5 s); a key Kernel refuses (401) is `422`, reason `refused_by_provider`. `KERNEL_API_KEY` is on the launcher's secrets list, and reaches a run's process over its pipe only when its revision names `browser`.
  - `GET /v2/capabilities`' `browser` tool gives its `providers`, the server's `provider` and what it `resolves` to, as the web tools do; `GET /v2/agents/{id}`' `tools` gives an agent's. With no vendor's keys it is unavailable with that reason, and no longer lists `missing` variables: either vendor's keys would do. The served contract marks `features.browser`'s `kernel` (`browser/1`).
- An agent's browser stays signed in: sign in once in one of its runs, and every later run of that agent starts from that signed-in browser. Sign-ins are kept per agent, never shared between agents.
  - The cookies stay at the browser's vendor, in a Browserbase context or a Kernel profile (made under a random name, `agentrun-` and 24 hex digits, since Kernel's profile names are its whole account's). The server keeps one pointer per agent and vendor, sealed under `AGENTRUN_SEAL_KEY` and bound to its agent, in the agent's home under `sign-ins/`, a host-owned folder the home API neither reads nor writes (`capabilities.home.host_owned` lists it). With no seal key nothing is kept. A local Chrome keeps none.
  - The server makes the agent's Browserbase context or Kernel profile when it has none (neither opens a browser) and hands a run the pointer over its pipe, never in its environment; the run's process never gets the seal key, and the model never sees the pointer.
  - The first run of an agent to start with the browser holds the save lease, and its sessions save. A run of the same agent started meanwhile starts signed in and saves nothing. The lease ends when the run ends or lets go to wait for its person; a run whose process went keeps it through its relaunch.
  - `GET /v2/agents/{id}` lists `sign_ins` (`key`, `provider`, `state` ready or held, `held_by`, `verified_at`), never the pointer, and `unreadable` (this server's own state, `signins/1`) for one that no longer opens under the seal key, which a run never replaces by itself; `capabilities.features.sign_ins` is true with a seal key and a browser vendor. `DELETE /v2/agents/{id}/sign-ins/{key}` (this server's own route) deletes the vendor's context or profile, best effort, and the pointer; it is `409 sign_in_held` while a run holds the lease.
  - V1 limits: the save lease goes to the first run with the browser in its tools, whether or not it browses, until that run ends; a run still going when its sign-in is forgotten keeps the pointer it was handed, and its later sessions fail.
- **A Daytona box reaches the package registries and GitHub, through Daytona's domain list**
  - pi-transport: `daytonaBoxes` takes `network: { allow: [...] }` when every entry is a domain (a `*.` wildcard allowed), sends it as Daytona's `domainAllowList`, and reports `network: "allowlist"` only once the sandbox's own read carries that list. A sandbox whose read lacks it is deleted and the create refused as `unsupported`, as `networkBlockAll` is checked. A CIDR in the list is still refused.
  - serve: a run's Daytona box gets the hosts `AGENTRUN_BOX_ALLOW` names. Unset, `pypi.org`, `files.pythonhosted.org`, `registry.npmjs.org`, `registry.yarnpkg.com`, `github.com` and `codeload.github.com`; a comma list replaces them; `none` blocks the network as before. `*`, an IP address or a CIDR refuses the start. Where Daytona keeps or takes no domain list (its tier 1 and 2), the box is made again with its network blocked, never open, and capabilities say why. Capabilities show the hosts in `places.allow` and each Daytona box's `network`.
- The box is a new agent's default machine. A new agentic agent whose definition names no place is saved in the Docker box wherever the server has docker, whether ready or still preparing its image (the first run waits for the image). The place is written on its revision. If the definition names no tools either, the agent gets `bash`, so it has a shell. A definition that names its tools keeps exactly those. Where docker is unavailable, or the definition names `browser` (runner-only for now), a new agent is saved as before and runs on the runner. `capabilities.places.default_place` says what a new agent gets, and `default_reason` says why when that is the runner. `places.default` stays `runner`, because it is where a revision that names no place runs. Agents saved before keep running where they ran, and a workflow is never given a box. The console's new-agent form starts on the served default, with bash ticked in a sandbox and the browser left unticked there.
- AgentRun Server runs OpenCode as a harness: a revision with `harness: "opencode"` runs on the pinned `opencode serve` (`opencode-ai` 1.19.5, an exact optional peer of the server that the host installs), started for each drive of the run's session, with the host's tools as its only tools and its model behind the session's proxy, durable in the run's own store. Its model goes where codex's does: the gateway at `AGENT_MODEL_BASE_URL`, or OpenAI with `OPENAI_API_KEY`, on the Responses API.
  - What a host does differently: install `opencode-ai@1.19.5` beside the server to make `opencode` available in `GET /v2/capabilities`. Without it, capabilities say it is not installed and give the install line.
  - OpenCode's own events for the session are mirrored into the run's store and given back to a new OpenCode process (`/sync/replay`) on each drive, so a follow-up, a stop or a crash resumes from the store. A turn a crash cut off gets the kernel's recovery note.
  - An OpenAI key saved in Settings → Model keys runs opencode too: with no `AGENT_MODEL` it gives opencode its default model, as it does codex. An OpenRouter key alone runs opencode on OpenAI's models through OpenRouter's Responses API (default `openai/gpt-6-sol`), the route codex takes. Under a spend cap each opencode request is sent with the output tokens what is left of the cap can buy, as codex's are.
  - The console offers OpenCode beside Pi, Claude and Codex when the server lists it.
  - An answer the model provider's content filter stops ends an opencode run `failed` with `moderated` after one request, as on the other harnesses: the session's model proxy reads the stop (OpenAI's API, or a gateway that sends each event as its data alone) and the drive ends the turn, so OpenCode never asks for the refused answer again.
  - An opencode run reserves 704 MB of the host's memory (measured: OpenCode's own process takes about 500 MB), so a 1 GB instance holds one opencode run at a time and a host under 832 MB doesn't offer it, with why.
- On Linux, a Docker box reaches a list of hosts, and nothing else: by default `pypi.org`, `files.pythonhosted.org`, `registry.npmjs.org`, `registry.yarnpkg.com`, `github.com` and `codeload.github.com`, so `pip install` and `npm install` work in it. `AGENTRUN_BOX_ALLOW` replaces the list with a comma list of names (where `*.example.com` names the hosts below `example.com`); `AGENTRUN_BOX_ALLOW=none` is the box offline, as before.
  - The box stays `--network none`. The run's process starts the kernel's egress proxy with the list, on a Unix socket in a directory of its own under `/tmp/agentrun-egress-<uid>/`, bound into the box read-only at `/run/agentrun/egress/`. A `socat` in the box relays `127.0.0.1:3128` to it, and `HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY`, `http_proxy`, `https_proxy`, `NO_PROXY` and `no_proxy` name it in the box's fixed environment. A program that skips them has no route out.
  - A host off the list, an IP address and any port but 80 and 443 are refused before any lookup: `403 <host> is not on this box's network list: use web_fetch for web pages, or ask the operator to add it to AGENTRUN_BOX_ALLOW`. A listed name whose address is private, this machine's or a cloud's metadata is refused too. The proxy holds no key and adds none.
  - The server decides once, at start, and fails closed: the box has the list only when this machine is Linux, the docker CLI's endpoint is a local unix socket, and `docker info` answers within 5 s with Linux containers on this machine's own kernel, keeping its data in a directory this server sees. Anything else (a Docker on another kernel such as Docker Desktop on a Mac or OrbStack, a daemon elsewhere, this server in a container given the host's docker socket, docker info that fails or does not answer) leaves the box with no network, and says why in `network_reason`, naming what it found.
  - A box made with another network than the server's (made under `none`, then the list turned on) is made again at its run's next open, not reused offline. The box's relay is waited on until it listens (up to 3 s); one that can't start (no socat or setsid in the image, or not listening) leaves the box with no network and one timeline row that says why.
  - Each start removes the proxy directories of runs that are gone (a socket that refuses a connect); a live run's stays.
  - Capabilities give the list as `places.allow`, and each box's `network`: `allowlist` or `none`, with `network_reason` when an available box has none. The start's banner says the same.
  - `*`, an IP address, a CIDR or a URL in the setting refuses the start, naming it.
  - The default box image adds `npm` and `socat`, so it builds once more. An image with no `socat` gets no network, and the run's timeline says so once.
- A run that its spend cap stops for room now pauses instead of failing. When what is left of `max_cost_usd` cannot buy the next request's least, the request is still not sent, so the cap holds, and the run ends up as follows:
  - It is `input_required` and `waiting`, with `error` null and `pending: {kind: "budget", cap_usd, spent_usd, needs_usd, said, at}`. `spent_usd` is what the cap counted as taken, and `needs_usd` is the least the refused request needs. They are kept on the refusal's start mark, so a restarted server reads them too.
  - Its timeline shows a waiting row: "Paused at the run's $1 cap: Not sent: …".
  - The webhook's `run.needs_input` carries `pending.kind: "budget"`.
  - Its process is let go while it waits, so it holds no place. A restarted server reads it paused.
  - A message to a paused run is `409 budget_reached` (a new error code). A cancel ends it.
  - The MCP tools say so too: `runs_get`'s `pending` names the budget kind, and `runs_respond` and the instructions say a run paused at its cap waits for its cap to be raised and is not answered.
  - The built-in console shows a paused run's reason ("Paused at its $X spend cap") in Mission Control and on its page. It offers no answer form and no composer there.

  A refusal no cap fixes still ends the run `failed` with `spend_cap_reached`: an unknown spend, a model with no price or no output ceiling, or an input that could not be measured. So does a cost found past the cap after a response. Raising the cap of a paused run comes next.
- `PATCH /runs/{run_id}` with `{max_cost_usd}` raises a run's spend cap, and only raises it. The new cap must be above the run's current cap, its last raise included, by more than 1e-9, and the same raise sent again (a retry, within 1e-9) changes nothing. A raise serve.json could not keep is not taken. It is `422` otherwise, and for a run with no cap or a workflow run; an ended run is `409 not_continuable`.
  - **Where the cap is kept:** the run's serve.json keeps the new cap, with each raise in `cap_raised`. The run's next process opens under it, and so does a server started again.
  - **A run paused at its cap goes on.** The answer is `{resumed: true, message_id}` at once. The pause gets one follow-up turn, "The cap was raised to $X; continue the task.", under that `message_id`, sent once the process being let go is gone; the turn runs under the new cap. A second raise before it goes answers the same id and raises what it says.
  - **Sent once, or the run fails saying why:** a server started again sends a follow-up that had not gone, to the process a launch started too. One that cannot be sent is tried again twice, then the run ends `failed` in plain words. A raised run's paused process that exits without releasing the run does not fail it. The follow-up shows in the timeline as the server's, "AgentRun: The cap was raised…", never as the person's.
  - **What a paused run has spent:** its figures are shown to a millionth of a dollar, as `cost.usd` is. Once its process is gone, `pending.spent_usd` is the store's spend when that is lower than the refusal's figure, which counted requests still answering at what it held for them. `suggested_cap_usd` follows it.
  - **The raise to offer:** a paused run's `pending` (`kind: "budget"`) adds `suggested_cap_usd`. It is `spent_usd` + 10 × `needs_usd`, rounded up to a whole dollar, and always above the run's cap, so a `PATCH` with it resumes the run. This is part of the `cappause/1` widening.
  - **Within a float's tail:** a request whose least, with what the run has spent, makes its cap to within a float's own tail (1e-12) fits it, but a spend past the cap by any more, however little, does not. The same holds for a paid call's check and for a cost read after a response. A request past the cap names a raise a cent above it, never the cap itself. Two caps within 1e-9 are the same cap.
  - **A working run's live process** keeps the cap it was opened with until its next process, and the answer's `note` says so.
- A run whose agent learned a workflow runs it as one tool, `learned_workflow {name, input}`, on pi, claude-agent and codex, with no edit of the agent's definition. A draft whose call node calls a tool set to ask or deny (a composed workflow's inner one too) is refused at the merge and before a call, in the words a workflow revision's call node is, the end-of-run prompt offers only tools set to allow, and each call node asks the run's gate again as it runs, so a tool set to deny or ask since the call began is refused there, never run. The tool gate holds it like any tool: `learned_workflow` is its settings key, allowed by default (the agent's own graded workflow, each of its nodes checked), and an agent's or the server's tool settings may set it to ask (the run parks until an owner answers, and an allow runs it once) or deny. Only a workflow whose `README.md` sets no status (or `active`) is the run's: one held for its owner's OK, or retired, is not. The tool's description names each workflow with its README's line, 8 KiB of lines in all, and the run's pin names the ones it gets (`workflows`), each kept whole in the run's directory, and the ones over that budget or holding a secret (`workflows_left_out`); the list is fixed when the run starts, so an approval or a retire takes effect at the next run, and a README whose front matter does not read one way is not read. A call checks the workflow again against the agent's own tools as its run resolves them and refuses one whose call node names a tool the agent was not granted, whose model node names any tool (the merge refuses that too), that holds a node its runner cannot run (a judge, pick or sift node needs a decision provider a run is not given; the merge refuses that too), whose agent node asks what its runner refuses when the node comes up (more than 8 turns, or context fork; the merge refuses those too), or that holds a code node or a shell or executor call, before any of its nodes runs. It runs with the workflow DSL: each model node on the run's own model, in a session of its own with no tool of its own, and each call node `via: "tool"` on a tool the agent has, its paid requests booked under the call. Its model requests are the run's, under its cap and on its timeline as `learned_workflow` side requests (on claude-agent and codex against one total with the harness's own, and on the harness's own route: on OpenRouter alone, through OpenRouter; claude-agent through the operator's own gateway at `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` is offered no learned workflow, nor is any harness at an origin its requests could not follow, and its pin says why in `workflows_off`; opencode takes codex's route), and a call a crash cut off is never run again.
- A paid tool call the spend cap refuses for room pauses the run, as a refused model request does: monid's run, Parallel's search and fetch, and browser time, on pi, claude-agent, codex and opencode.
  - **The call is not sent.** Its result says so in the cap's words, and the model's next request is refused in those words, so the turn ends there and the model gets no turn to work around it.
  - **The run waits on a raise:** `input_required`, `waiting`, `pending.kind: "budget"`, with what was taken, the call's hold as what it needs, and the raise it is offered. A raise sends it on, and the model makes the call again if it still needs it, once per call it makes.
  - **A stop between the refusal and the next request**, then a message, starts a turn judged as any other.
  - **A spend that is unknown still fails the run**, as for a model request.
- The runs lists (`GET /v2/runs` and `GET /v2/agents/{agent_id}/runs`) name the trigger that started each run, as the run's own view does: `trigger: {id, firing_key}`, or `null` for a run a caller started. An ended run listed after a restart reads it from the `serve.json` its list row already reads, so the list makes no extra read.
- A schedule tells you when it misses or fails. Of the times missed while the server was down, the latest still fires, once and marked `catch_up`, when it is within the trigger's `catchup_window_s` (default 3600; 0 never catches up), and the rest stay one `missed` firing with their count.
  A firing whose run is refused sends a `trigger.failed` webhook message, signed like the run notices, with `data: {trigger_id, name, firing_key, nominal_at, error, auto_paused}`; its `webhook-id` is made from the firing's key, so a restart never sends it twice. Five failed firings in a row turn the trigger off with `disabled: {reason: "auto_paused"}`, and the fifth's message says so; a firing that starts its run resets `failures_in_a_row`, and so does turning the trigger on again. A webhook receiver now sees this third message type when a trigger fails; capabilities list it in `features.webhooks.events`.
  Overlap stays `skip` alone: a firing while the last run is active (working, or parked waiting on a person) is skipped with why, never queued, because a firing queued behind a parked run would wait as long as the person does.
- Schedules start an agent's capped runs on their own: `POST /v2/triggers` with a source of `{kind: "schedule", cron, timezone}` (five fields, an IANA zone), `{kind: "schedule", at}` (once; the trigger turns itself off after) or `{kind: "schedule", every_s, start_at?}` (60 to 31622400, a year and a day, on the grid from `start_at`), and a target of `{kind: "start_run", agent_id, input, max_cost_usd, effort?}`. `max_cost_usd` is required, and a workflow agent is refused, since a workflow run's spend is not capped here.
  Each firing starts its run once, across retries and restarts: its key comes from the trigger and its nominal time, and its run is started under that key. A firing while the trigger's last run is still active (working, or waiting on a person) is recorded as skipped, with why. Times missed while the server was down are one `missed` firing with their count; the schedule goes on from now. `PATCH /v2/triggers/{id}` with `enabled: false` pauses it, and the time it was off is skipped, not caught up. `POST /v2/triggers/{id}/fire` runs it now, once per `Idempotency-Key`. `GET /v2/triggers/{id}/firings` lists its last 200 firings, and a run it started names it in `trigger`. Triggers live in `triggers.json` in the data directory; capabilities list them under `features.triggers`.
- On `pi`, `codex` and `opencode`, a run that reads its files (the `read` or `bash` tool) gets its skills on demand, as `claude-agent` does through its Skill tool: its system prompt names each skill, what it is for and its `SKILL.md`'s path under `.agents/skills/`, and the agent reads the file when the skill fits the step it is on. The revision's own skills and the ones the agent learned alike. A run with neither tool gets every `SKILL.md` inlined, as before.
   A skill's front matter description is read whole as YAML folds it: one that wraps onto indented lines, or one written entirely on the next lines.
- **An approved MCP call runs once through the executor its gateway registers**
  - The tool gate keeps an executor for each tool prefix (`executor("mcp:", run)`, which the MCP gateway registers). Once the owner allows a parked `mcp:<server>/<tool>` call, the gate runs it through that executor exactly once, on the same journaled path as a kernel host tool: the call's intent is in the run's store before it runs, and a call a crash cut off is answered interrupted, never run again. The run resumes with its answer, a failure included, through the gate's one resume message.
  - An approved call whose prefix has no executor is not run, and the run resumes being told so: `The approved <tool> call was not run: nothing in this run runs mcp: calls.`
- **Every tool call passes one gate: each tool is allow, ask or deny, and an ask parks the run until the owner answers**
  - `permission` (`allow`, `ask`, `deny`) in `tool_settings`, the agent's own over the server's (`PUT /v2/settings/tools`). An MCP server's wildcard, `mcp:<server>/*`, sets each of its tools, under a tool's own key. By default every built-in tool is allowed and a catalog tool (`monid.<tool>`, `mcp:<server>/<tool>`) asks. So until monid's tools are set to `allow`, an agent that calls them waits for the owner at each call; on a server with accounts off (`AGENTRUN_ACCOUNTS=0`) each call is refused instead, and so is a workflow model node's. `ask` is offered for `read`, `write`, `edit`, `bash`, `fetch_page`, `web_fetch`, `web_search` and catalog tools; any value not offered is `422`. `features.tool_permissions` is now `true`. `capabilities.tools` give each tool's `permission`, `default_permission` and `permissions`, and `GET /v2/agents/{id}`'s `tools` add `permission_from`.
  - pi's tools, and claude-agent's and codex's host tools, all pass the gate. `deny` answers the call at once with an error, and the run goes on. `ask` ends the turn: the run is `input_required` and `waiting`, with `pending: {kind: "approval", id, tool, call_id, args_preview, requested_at}` on the run and its summary, a `tool_approval_requested` timeline row after the call's result, and a `run.needs_input` webhook with `pending.kind` `approval`. `POST /v2/runs/{id}/approvals/{approval_id}` with `{decision, note?}` answers it, from the owner's password session only: a bearer token is `403`. On allow the kernel runs the call once, on the run's own coding source, and resumes the session with one message that names the call and carries its result; deny resumes it with the refusal. The same answer again is `204`, another `409` (`already_answered`). The request survives a restart, and a cancel marks it canceled.
  - Under `ask`, claude-agent's and codex's own web search, which runs inside the CLI where no call can wait, gives way to the kernel's (`native` picked: unavailable). Under `deny`, `web_search` and `web_fetch` resolve `unavailable: denied`, and the run still starts.
  - On a server with accounts off (`AGENTRUN_ACCOUNTS=0`) no one can answer an approval, so an `ask` is refused at each call (`Permission denied: <tool> needs the owner's approval, and this server has accounts off (AGENTRUN_ACCOUNTS=0), so no one can give it: set the tool to allow or deny in the agent's Tools.`), never parked, and `capabilities.tools` give `ask_needs: "password"` on each tool `ask` is offered for.
  - The learning lane's end-of-run turn writes no file under `work/.learning/`: its write or edit there is refused (`A note is kept only from your answer: write it in a ```note block, not to a file.`), whatever the tool's permission, since its notes are kept only from its answer. Any other turn's write there follows the tool's permission. The refusal guides the model and is not a security boundary: it reads the path as written, so a write through a link or a shell redirect is not refused. Nothing depends on it, since the host lays `.learning/` from its own store and never reads back a file the run wrote there.
  - A workflow call node that calls a tool set to deny refuses its run (`409 agent_not_runnable`, `details.denied`), and so does one set to ask (`details.asks`), since a call node cannot wait for an approval. A tool switched to deny or ask while the run goes refuses its next call node in the gate's words. A model node's asked call is refused, since no node can wait.
  - Upgrading: a workflow whose call node's tool is set to ask, by a setting or a default, now refuses its run (`409`, `details.asks`): set that tool to allow for the agent (`definition.tool_settings`). Built-in tools are allowed by default; catalog (`monid.*`) and MCP tools, which ask by default, are no call node's tools here.
  - `@parcha/pi-durable-agent`: a revision's `toolSettings` keeps a catalog tool's name (`monid.run`), an MCP server's tool (`mcp:<server>/<tool>`) and its wildcard (`mcp:<server>/*`) as keys, beside a plain name; its timeline's `TimelineKind` declares `tool_approval_requested`.
- A signed webhook starts or messages an agent. A trigger with `source: {kind: "webhook"}` fires on each `POST /v2/hooks/{trigger_id}` signed by its own secret under the Standard Webhooks spec (`webhook-id`, `webhook-timestamp` within 5 minutes, `webhook-signature`), checked on the raw body before anything else; it is the server's one route with no token. A bad signature, a stale timestamp, an unknown trigger, one that is off and one that is not a webhook's all answer the same `401`; a body past 256 KiB is `413`. The secret is made with the trigger, shown in its `201` once, kept sealed in `trigger-secrets.json` (no seal key, no webhook trigger: `409 no_sealing_key`), cut by an import, and rotated with `POST /v2/triggers/{id}/secret`.
  The `webhook-id` is the firing's key: the first request answers `202` once its firing is recorded, and the same id again answers `200` with that firing and starts nothing. The agent gets the trigger's input, then the body, cut at 16 KiB, inside `<trigger-payload source="webhook" untrusted>`. A new target, `{kind: "message_run", run_id, input}`, sends each firing (from a webhook or a schedule) to one run as a message, under the firing's key, which the run's store takes once; the run must have a spend cap, and a firing never answers a run that waits on its person (it is skipped). A trigger holds at most 20 firings due (a firing to a working run waits for it): past them a webhook event and a run now answer `429` with `Retry-After`, recording nothing, and a schedule's time is skipped. A webhook trigger's create retried under its Idempotency-Key answers the same trigger with a note in place of the secret. A note that would keep a webhook signing secret (`whsec_`, this server's or any other's) is refused by learning's checks. Capabilities list `sources: ["schedule", "webhook"]` and `features.triggers.webhook`.
- A message the server writes into a run, a raised cap's follow-up or an approval's resume, can carry its origin
  (`{ kind: "server", reason: "cap_raised" | "approval" }`, or `"approval_declined"` with `said`, the words of the person's
  message that declined the call). The Timeline then shows it as a phase row, "AgentRun: <its first line>", never as
  "You: …", and a declined approval's words as the person's own row beside it; the model reads the same text. A run door's `POST /submit` takes `origin`. On a harness run the
  origin is written on the message's user entry. On pi, whose user entry carries no data, a marker entry
  (`agentrun.message_origin`, its text) goes first, and the Timeline matches it to the next user entry with that exact text.
  The server's row carries `origin: "server"` (a new optional field of a Timeline row), and it counts, as a person's
  message does, as the message its turn took: serve reads a run's messages (their arrival, where a stop stands) through one
  predicate. pi-durable-agent's timeline exports `MessageOrigin`, `MESSAGE_ORIGIN`, `originOf`, `messageOrigins` (the one
  reader) and `isMessageRow`, and pi-durable-disk exports `MESSAGE_ORIGIN_KIND`. A door's `POST /submit` takes exactly the
  reasons `cap_raised`, `approval` and `approval_declined`.
- A tool's key the server is started with (Browserbase's, Parallel's, monid's, TypeSafe's) is checked once as the server starts, with the same call that costs nothing a saved key gets, in the background, so the start never waits on it. `GET /v2/settings/tool-keys` shows its `check`: `accepted`, `not_checked` and why, or `refused` (`Browserbase refused BROWSERBASE_API_KEY (401: Unauthorized) when this server started: set the right one where AgentRun starts, then restart it`). A tool that needs a refused key (the browser on Browserbase's, monid's tools on monid's) reads unavailable in capabilities with the same words, instead of available until a run fails.
- Runs execute in a Modal sandbox (box `modal`). An agent whose place is `modal` gets its `bash`, `read`, `write` and `edit` in a Modal sandbox, the way a Daytona box works. It is Modal's default sandbox runtime (gVisor), not a virtual machine. The server holds the token and the SDK client, makes the sandbox, seeds it with the run's `work/`, copies what changed back at each turn's end and before the run's end, and ends it at the run's end, a cancel or an archive. A sandbox that is gone is made again on the next call. The run's process reaches its sandbox only through the server's box relay, which makes only that run's own tool calls. The Modal sandbox has no network: Modal takes a host list, but the server cannot read a sandbox's list back to check that it holds, so each box is made blocked, and capabilities say so in `network_reason`. It takes the CPU and memory limits a Docker box takes: 2 CPUs and 4 GiB by default, which `AGENTRUN_BOX_CPUS` and `AGENTRUN_BOX_MEMORY` change.
- The background tools no longer trust files in `work/background/`, which the run's own file tools can write. Before, a pid written there was a group `bash_signal` and the run's end would signal (a forged `1` meant every process in the box), and a link planted at `output.log`, `command`, `started` or `exit` read another file of the box. A link planted at `ended` was a file the run's end truncated.
  - The group, the exit code, the start time, the command and the run's-end mark are now kept in the box's own directory, out of `work/`. The files in `work/background/<id>/` are display copies that no call reads back, each written in the box's own directory and moved over its name from inside `work/background/<id>`, which is entered and checked by its real path. A link planted at the name or at the directory is never followed.
  - `output.log` is opened first and checked as opened: it must be the log itself by its real path, and a regular file. Every read is then of that open file, so a link swapped in after the check is never read. A link planted at it is refused in words.
  - A directory made in `work/background/` that the box did not start reads as gone and is never signalled.
